- FX exposure taxonomy: Transaction (contractual cash flows in foreign
  currency), Translation (consolidation of foreign subsidiaries),
  Economic (competitive exposure to FX)
- Hedging policy design: what % of transaction exposure to hedge
  (typically 50–80% for 12-month horizon); permitted hedging instruments
  (forwards, options, cross-currency swaps)
- Natural hedging identification: revenue and cost in the same currency
  reduces the need for financial hedging
- Hedge accounting under ASC 815 / IFRS 9: cash flow hedge designation
  requirements and P&L volatility reduction benefit

Interest Rate Risk Management:
- Fixed vs. floating debt mix: optimal fixed/floating ratio based on
  the company's financial profile and macro rate outlook
- Interest rate swap strategy: paying fixed to convert floating debt to
  fixed; receiver swaps for flexible rate management
- Debt refinancing timing: how to choose between accessing the market now
  vs. waiting; refinancing decision framework

PROMPT 75 — The BlackRock Enterprise Risk Management (ERM) Framework Designer
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at BlackRock's Risk & Quantitative Analysis group
who designs Enterprise Risk Management frameworks for large financial
institutions and non-financial corporates seeking to embed risk management
into strategic decision-making.

I need a comprehensive ERM framework that integrates strategic, financial,
operational, and ESG risk management across the enterprise.

Design the ERM framework:

Risk Governance & Culture:
- Three Lines of Defense model: Business (owns and manages risk) →
  Risk Management function (sets policy, provides oversight) →
  Internal Audit (independent assurance); define roles at each line
- Risk appetite statement: how to articulate and calibrate the firm's
  risk appetite in quantitative and qualitative terms; link risk appetite
  to strategic objectives
- Risk culture assessment: 5 indicators of a strong risk culture
  (tone from the top, psychological safety, incentive alignment, near-miss
  reporting, risk-adjusted performance management)

Risk Identification & Assessment:
- Enterprise risk taxonomy: Financial Risks (market, credit, liquidity),
  Operational Risks (process, people, technology, legal), Strategic Risks
  (competitive, M&A, reputational), ESG Risks (climate, social, governance)
- Risk heat map construction: probability × impact matrix (5×5 grid);
  velocity (how quickly a risk can materialize) as a third dimension
- Emerging risk identification: horizon-scanning process; scenario
  planning for tail risks not yet in the heat map
- Key Risk Indicators (KRIs): identify the 15 leading indicators that
  provide early warning across the top enterprise risks; trigger levels
  and escalation protocol

Quantitative Risk Management:
- Value-at-Risk (VaR): methodology (historical simulation, Monte Carlo,
  parametric); limitations and what VaR does NOT tell you
- Stress testing: CCAR-style stress scenario design for non-financial
  companies; reverse stress testing (what scenario would threaten
  the company's survival?)
- Risk-adjusted performance: RAROC (Risk-Adjusted Return on Capital)
  framework for comparing business units on an apples-to-apples basis

Risk Reporting & Board Oversight:
- Board risk report design: quarterly risk dashboard for the Board's
  Risk Committee; what the board needs to see vs. what management
  tracks; frequency and format

Format as a BlackRock ERM Framework Design Document with:
  • Three Lines of Defense Model
  • Risk Heat Map (5×5 matrix description)

  • Stress Test Scenario Library
  • Board Risk Report Template

PROMPT 76 — The Goldman Sachs Corporate Derivatives & Commodity Hedging Advisor
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director in Goldman Sachs' Global Markets division who
structures commodity and financial derivatives hedging programs for
corporate clients in energy, airlines, consumer goods, and industrials.

I need a comprehensive commodity and financial hedging strategy to protect
margins and reduce earnings volatility from commodity price, FX, and
interest rate risk.

Design the hedging program:

Commodity Risk Analysis:
- Commodity exposure mapping: for each commodity input (oil, gas, metals,
  agricultural commodities), quantify:
  • Annual consumption volume
  • Dollar exposure per $10/unit move in commodity price
  • As % of annual COGS and EBITDA
- Correlation to finished product pricing: does the company have
  natural pass-through (price increases permitted) or is it a price
  taker with fixed customer contracts?
- Basis risk: the risk that the hedging instrument (e.g., WTI crude futures)
  does not perfectly track the actual commodity purchased (e.g., Brent crude
  in Europe); quantify and manage basis risk

Hedging Instrument Selection:
- For each risk type, compare instruments on: cost, flexibility, accounting
  treatment, and credit requirements:
  (1) Exchange-traded futures: standardized, margined, liquid, basis risk
  (2) OTC forwards: customized, no daily margin, counterparty credit risk
  (3) Fixed-price swaps: synthetic fixed price purchase
  (4) Collars (buy put + sell call): cap/floor with zero net premium
  (5) Asian options: average price hedges for companies with monthly
      consumption profiles
  (6) Producer hedging (prepay contracts with suppliers)

Hedging Program Design:
- Hedging horizon and ratio: recommend % of exposure to hedge at each
  time horizon (e.g., 80% of 12-month forward consumption, 60% of
  13–24 months, 30% of 25–36 months); rationale for layered approach
- Hedge accounting qualification: designate as cash flow hedge under
  ASC 815 to avoid P&L mark-to-market volatility; effectiveness testing
- Credit support: CSA (Credit Support Annex) negotiation for OTC
  derivatives; threshold, minimum transfer amounts, and collateral types
- Cost-benefit analysis: expected savings from hedging vs. cost of
  premium and opportunity cost (foregone upside)

Format as a Goldman Sachs Commodity Hedging Advisory Memo with:
  • Commodity Exposure Quantification Table
  • Hedging Instrument Comparison Matrix
  • Recommended Hedging Program (by commodity, tenor, and instrument)
  • Hedge Accounting Treatment Summary
  • Annual P&L Volatility Reduction Estimate (hedged vs. unhedged)

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | INDUSTRY | PRIMARY COMMODITY EXPOSURES |
            ANNUAL COMMODITY SPEND | CURRENT HEDGING POLICY (IF ANY) |
            BIGGEST HEDGING CONCERN | HEDGE ACCOUNTING APPETITE]

PROMPT 77 — The Ares Management Direct Lending Credit Underwriting Framework
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Ares Management's Credit Group — the world's
largest publicly traded credit manager — who underwrites direct lending
investments to middle-market companies (EBITDA $10M–$75M) typically backing
private equity sponsors.

I need a comprehensive direct lending credit underwriting framework covering
credit analysis, deal structuring, and portfolio management.

Underwrite the credit:

Business Quality Assessment:
- Revenue and EBITDA quality: % of recurring revenue, contract duration,
  customer concentration, revenue cyclicality, and EBITDA add-back
  legitimacy (flag aggressive add-backs)
- Competitive position: niche market leadership (a key direct lending
  criterion — typically top-2 in a defensible niche market)
- Management team assessment: sponsor track record with this management
  team; management equity rollover as an alignment signal
- Sponsor quality: tier-ranking the PE sponsor (Tier 1: large established
  PE firms; Tier 2: mid-market specialists; Tier 3: smaller or first-time
  funds) — how does sponsor quality affect pricing and covenant protection?

Credit Metrics & Structuring:
- Entry leverage: Total Debt/EBITDA; Senior Secured Debt/EBITDA — compare
  to sector standards; coverage ratios: EBITDA/Interest, EBITDA−Capex/
  Interest, FCF/Debt Service
- Loan structure design: first-lien senior secured (most common in direct
  lending), unitranche, second lien, or mezzanine; explain the risk/return
  trade-off of each
- Pricing: reference rate (SOFR) + credit spread; upfront fees (OID);
  PIK toggle option and when it's appropriate; expected all-in yield
- Covenant package: maintenance covenants (Total Leverage, Interest
  Coverage, Minimum Liquidity — set at meaningful headroom levels);
  incurrence covenants (anti-accordion basket, acquisition limitations)
  — importance of maintenance covenants in direct lending vs. cov-lite
  broadly syndicated loans
- Collateral package: first priority lien on all assets (real property,
  IP, accounts receivable, inventory); borrowing base if applicable

Scenario & Stress Analysis:
- Base case: EBITDA grows at sponsor model; loan fully repaid at exit
- Stress case: EBITDA declines 20%; model DSCR breach timing and recovery
  value in a restructuring (liquidation value vs. going-concern value)
- Loan-to-Value (LTV) analysis: enterprise value cushion above the loan
  amount at entry; minimum acceptable entry LTV threshold

Format as an Ares Direct Lending Investment Committee Memo with:
  • Credit Metrics Summary (Leverage, Coverage, LTV)
  • Covenant Package Recommendation
  • Stress Test Scenario Table
  • Expected Return Analysis (Base / Stress → IRR and Loss Given Default)
  • Portfolio Fit Assessment (Sector concentration, sponsor concentration)

My inputs: [BORROWER NAME | INDUSTRY | SPONSOR | LTM EBITDA | PROPOSED LOAN
            AMOUNT | PROPOSED LEVERAGE | BUSINESS DESCRIPTION | PRIMARY CREDIT RISK]

PROMPT 78 — The Blackstone Credit & Insurance (BXCI) Private Credit Portfolio Strategy
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Portfolio Manager at Blackstone Credit & Insurance (BXCI) —
one of the world's largest credit managers — responsible for constructing and
managing a diversified private credit portfolio for insurance company
general accounts, pension funds, and sovereign wealth funds.

I need a comprehensive private credit portfolio construction strategy
covering all sub-asset classes within the private credit universe.

Design the portfolio:

Private Credit Universe Mapping:
- Direct Lending: senior secured middle-market loans (EBITDA $10M–$75M
  backed by PE sponsors); typical return 10–12% all-in yield; floating rate
- Leveraged Loans (Broadly Syndicated): larger loans ($500M+) to larger
  sponsors; more liquid but tighter spreads (7–9% all-in)
- High Yield Bonds: fixed rate, public, liquid; 6–9% yield
- Mezzanine / Subordinated Debt: junior debt behind senior secured;
  12–15% return (cash + PIK); equity upside via warrants
- Real Estate Debt: commercial mortgage loans, CRE CLOs, CMBS B-pieces;
  targeted returns 8–12% depending on subordination
- Infrastructure Debt: investment-grade senior secured project finance
  and infrastructure loans; 4–7% yield with very low default rates
- Specialty Finance: consumer loans, SME loans, receivables finance,
  CLOs — asset-backed credit with structural protections

Allocation & Portfolio Construction:
- Return/risk/liquidity matrix: rank each sub-asset class on: expected
  net return, default rate, recovery rate, and liquidity (time to exit)
- Recommended portfolio weights for: (a) Insurance general account
  (capital efficiency, RBC charges per sub-asset class), (b) Pension
  fund (liability-matching, long duration preference), (c) SWF
  (pure return maximization with illiquidity premium capture)
- Vintage year diversification: importance of committing consistently
  across market cycles; avoid concentration in one vintage
- Correlation analysis: how do the sub-asset classes perform relative
  to each other in credit downturns (2008, 2020)?

Credit Cycle Positioning:
- Late cycle vs. early cycle positioning: which sub-asset classes to
  overweight/underweight at each phase of the credit cycle
- Defensive positioning signals: spread compression, covenant erosion,
  leverage creep — when these are observed, rotate to senior/secured
  and reduce subordinated exposure

Format as a BXCI Private Credit Portfolio Strategy with:
  • Sub-Asset Class Universe Table (Return / Risk / Liquidity Matrix)
  • Recommended Portfolio Weights by Investor Type
  • Vintage Year Diversification Strategy
  • Credit Cycle Positioning Guide
  • Expected Portfolio Yield, Duration, and Default Rate

PROMPT 79 — The Golub Capital Unitranche & One-Stop Financing Structuring Advisor
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Golub Capital — one of the most respected
direct lenders in the US middle market — specializing in unitranche and
one-stop financing solutions for PE-backed leveraged buyouts.

I need a comprehensive unitranche financing structure and credit analysis
for a PE-backed LBO transaction, explaining the unitranche product and
its advantages vs. a traditional senior/junior split-capital structure.

Structure and analyze:

Unitranche Mechanics:
- Definition: a single blended loan that combines first and second lien
  debt into one tranche with one interest rate and one lender relationship
- Agreement Among Lenders (AAL): how unitranche actually works — the
  synthetic split into "first out" (FO) and "last out" (LO) pieces within
  the unitranche, with different pricing and repayment priority
- Blended interest rate calculation: FO rate + LO rate weighted by size
  = effective blended rate for the borrower; show an example calculation
  (e.g., 60% FO at SOFR+500 + 40% LO at SOFR+800 = SOFR+620 blended)
- Comparison to traditional senior + mezz structure: same total leverage,
  but unitranche has: (a) one lender relationship, (b) faster execution,
  (c) flexible documentation, (d) typically covenant-lite below traditional
  direct lending thresholds

Unitranche Sizing & Terms:
- Typical sizing: 4.5x–6.0x EBITDA total unitranche; some one-stop
  providers go to 7.0x+ in hot markets for high-quality sponsors
- Pricing: current market all-in rate for unitranche by leverage tier
  (< 4.5x, 4.5–5.5x, > 5.5x) and EBITDA size
- Covenant package: single financial maintenance covenant (Leverage Ratio),
  typically set at 50–75bps headroom to closing leverage
- Accordion feature: uncommitted accordion that allows the borrower to
  upsize the facility post-close for acquisitions
- Equity commitment: PE sponsor equity check (typically 40–50% of total
  capitalization in direct lending deals)

Credit Underwriting for This Transaction:
- EBITDA quality assessment: identify top 5 add-backs and whether they
  are defensible; adjusted EBITDA vs. Golub's credit EBITDA (typically
  more conservative than sponsor EBITDA)
- Leverage analysis: entry leverage, DSCR, and required EBITDA growth
  to de-lever below 5.0x by Year 3
- Exit analysis: what PE sponsor exit scenarios generate full repayment
  plus fee income for Golub?

Format as a Golub Capital Unitranche Credit Memo with:
  • Unitranche vs. Senior/Junior Structure Comparison Table
  • Blended Rate Calculation Example
  • Deal Terms Summary (Sizing, Pricing, Covenant, Maturity)
  • Credit Metrics at Close and Year 3 Projection
  • Stress Analysis: EBITDA -20% → DSCR and Covenant Headroom

My inputs: [BORROWER NAME | SPONSOR | INDUSTRY | LTM EBITDA | PROPOSED LEVERAGE
            (X EBITDA) | ACQUISITION MULTIPLE | EQUITY % | BUSINESS DESCRIPTION]

PROMPT 80 — The Norway Government Pension Fund (GPFG) Sovereign Wealth Fund

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Portfolio Strategist at Norges Bank Investment Management
(NBIM) — which manages the $1.7 trillion Norway Government Pension Fund

  wealth transfer), reserve investment corporation, pension reserve fund,

  in investment decisions
- Cost management: why low cost is a competitive advantage for a SWF;
  GPFG's < 5bps total cost ratio as a benchmark

Format as a SWF Asset Allocation & Governance Blueprint with:
  • SWF Mandate & Spending Rule Design

PROMPT 81 — The Goldman Sachs Healthcare Investment Banking Biotech Valuation
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director in Goldman Sachs' Healthcare Investment Banking
group who has led $100B+ in biotech M&A, IPOs, and equity follow-ons for
companies spanning clinical-stage biotechs to large-cap pharma.

I need a rigorous biotech/pharma company valuation using the risk-adjusted
Net Present Value (rNPV) methodology — the industry-standard approach for
valuing drug pipelines.

Build the valuation:

rNPV Pipeline Valuation Methodology:
- Explain why traditional DCF fails for clinical-stage companies (no
  current revenue, binary outcomes, long development timelines) and
  why rNPV is the appropriate methodology
- For each pipeline asset, build an rNPV model:
  • Peak sales estimate: patient population × diagnosis rate × treatment
    rate × market share × price (net of rebates) × launch year
  • Revenue ramp: S-curve adoption model from launch to peak (typically
    3–5 years); plateau duration; loss-of-exclusivity (LOE) cliff
  • Cost of goods and operating expenses: COGS (10–30% for biologics),
    SG&A (20–30% of sales), and ongoing R&D for lifecycle management
  • Probability of Technical and Regulatory Success (PTRS) by phase:
    Phase 1→2: 60–70%, Phase 2→3: 30–40%, Phase 3→Approval: 50–70%,
    NDA/BLA Approval: 85–90%; compound all phases to get overall PoS
  • Discount rate: WACC for approved products (8–10%); higher for
    clinical-stage (12–15%) to reflect binary risk
  • NPV per asset × PTRS = rNPV per asset
  • Sum all pipeline rNPVs + net cash − debt = equity value

Comparable Biotech Valuation:
- EV/Pipeline rNPV multiple: compare to peer biotechs at similar stage
- Price/Sales for commercial-stage biotech: NTM and Year+2 P/S multiples
- Precedent M&A transactions: acquisition premiums paid for assets at
  each development stage (Phase 2, Phase 3, pre-NDA, commercial)

Catalyst & Binary Event Mapping:
- Map all upcoming binary events (clinical readouts, FDA decisions,
  PDUFA dates) with probability-weighted stock price scenarios
- Implied valuation for each event outcome: success vs. failure price
  target and the stock move expected in each scenario

Format as a Goldman Sachs Biotech Valuation Memo with:
  • rNPV Model by Pipeline Asset (table with all assumptions)
  • Sum-of-Parts Equity Value Waterfall
  • Comparable Company & Transaction Analysis
  • Catalyst Calendar with Probability-Weighted Scenarios
  • Investment Recommendation: Buy / Hold / Sell with Price Target

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | TICKER | PIPELINE ASSETS (INDICATION, PHASE) |
            NET CASH | ANNUAL BURN RATE | PRIMARY INVESTMENT CATALYST |
            BIGGEST RISK TO THE THESIS]

PROMPT 83 — The Leerink Partners Medtech & Life Sciences Equity Research Framework
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Equity Research Analyst at Leerink Partners — the leading
independent healthcare investment bank and research firm — covering medical
devices, diagnostics, and life sciences tools companies.

I need a comprehensive equity research framework for analyzing medtech and
life sciences tools companies with sector-specific valuation and KPI analysis.

Analyze:

Medtech Business Model & Quality Assessment:
- Revenue model classification: capital equipment + disposables/consumables
  (razor/razorblade model), pure consumables, software/services, or
  diagnostic test volumes — assess the recurring revenue % and its
  margin profile
- Installed base analysis: total installed base of capital equipment;
  annual replacement cycle; pull-through revenue per installed unit
  (the key revenue predictability metric for medtech)
- Clinical evidence quality: number of published clinical studies, level
  of evidence (RCT vs. observational), regulatory approval pathway
  (510(k) vs. PMA); reimbursement status (covered by CMS, commercial
  payors — CPT code and reimbursement rate)
- Market penetration: current penetration rate of target patient
  population; path to peak penetration; competitive products and
  market share dynamics
- Hospital capital budget sensitivity: is the product a "must have"
  or a "nice to have"? How does it perform in hospital capex freeze
  environments?

PROMPT 84 — The Evercore ISI Managed Care & Health Insurance Sector Analyst

PROMPT 92 — The Lazard Renewable Energy Project Finance & LCOE Analyzer
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Lazard's Power, Energy & Infrastructure group —
who publishes the annual Levelized Cost of Energy (LCOE) analysis, the global
standard for renewable energy economics — advising utilities, developers, and
infrastructure funds on renewable energy project financing.

I need a comprehensive renewable energy project finance analysis covering
LCOE, project economics, and optimal financing structure.

Analyze:

Levelized Cost of Energy (LCOE) Methodology:
- LCOE definition: the NPV of total lifecycle costs ÷ total lifetime
  energy production ($/MWh); makes different generation technologies
  comparable on a like-for-like basis
- LCOE components:
  (1) Capital Cost ($/kW installed): module/turbine cost, BOS
      (Balance of System: inverters, racking, wiring), EPC contractor
      margin, soft costs (permits, interconnection, development)
  (2) Operating & Maintenance Cost ($/MWh): fixed O&M ($/kW-year),
      variable O&M ($/MWh), insurance, land lease
  (3) Capacity Factor: % of hours per year the plant generates at
      full capacity — solar utility: 20–30%, onshore wind: 25–45%,
      offshore wind: 35–55%
  (4) Discount Rate (WACC): how to set WACC for renewable projects
      (lower than corporate WACC due to contracted cash flows)
  (5) Project Life: typically 25–35 years; degradation rate (solar:
      0.5% per year)
- LCOE comparison: solar, onshore wind, offshore wind, CCGT, nuclear,
  coal — current ranges and 10-year trajectory (learning curves)

Project Finance Structure:
- Non-recourse project finance: why projects use ring-fenced SPV
  structures; the role of the offtake agreement (PPA) in enabling
  low-cost debt financing
- Capital structure: typical 70:30 debt/equity split; senior secured
  project finance debt terms (DSCR covenant ≥ 1.25×, 15–20 year tenor)
- Revenue contract analysis: PPA terms ($/MWh fixed price or index-
  linked, duration, curtailment provisions); merchant revenue risk if
  partially uncontracted
- Tax equity: the critical role of US tax equity in ITC/PTC monetization
  (IRA enhanced credits); tax equity structure (partnership flip, sale-
  leaseback); effective return to tax equity investor (7–9% after-tax)
- Construction risk: EPC fixed-price lump-sum contract; performance
  bonds; delay liquidated damages; completion guarantee from sponsor

Returns Analysis:
- Project IRR (unlevered) and equity IRR (levered): target equity IRR
  for utility-scale solar (8–10%), onshore wind (10–12%), offshore wind
  (10–14%); DSCR sensitivity to merchant price assumptions
- Refinancing upside: "back-leverage" refinancing once construction risk
  is resolved typically increases equity returns 200–400bps

Format as a Lazard Renewable Energy Project Finance Memo with:
  • LCOE Build-Up Table (by technology, current and projected)
  • Project Finance Sources & Uses
  • Revenue Waterfall (PPA revenue → DSCR → equity cash flow)
  • Tax Equity Structure Diagram
  • Equity IRR Sensitivity (Capacity Factor × PPA Price)

My inputs: [PROJECT TYPE (SOLAR/WIND/STORAGE/HYBRID) | LOCATION | PROJECT SIZE
            (MW) | CAPITAL COST ($/W) | OFFTAKE STRUCTURE (PPA OR MERCHANT) |
            PPA PRICE IF APPLICABLE | TARGET EQUITY IRR]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 93 — The Goldman Sachs Energy Transition M&A & Corporate Strategy Advisor
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director in Goldman Sachs' Natural Resources Investment Banking
group who advises oil & gas majors (ExxonMobil, Shell, TotalEnergies, BP) and
independent E&P companies on energy transition strategy, portfolio optimization,
and clean energy M&A.

I need a comprehensive energy transition strategy for an oil & gas company
navigating decarbonization pressures while maximizing shareholder value.

Design the strategy:

Integrated Energy Company Strategy Framework:
- Business model options along the energy transition spectrum:
  (1) Integrated Energy Company (IEA): balanced portfolio of hydrocarbons
      + renewables + power + trading (Shell, TotalEnergies model)
  (2) Focused E&P with returns discipline: maximize FCF from hydrocarbons,
      return capital to shareholders, minimal low-carbon investment
      (ConocoPhillips, Pioneer model)
  (3) Radical transformation: divest hydrocarbons, pivot to renewables
      (Orsted, NextEra model — but requires access to low-cost capital)
  Evaluate each against: TSR potential, ESG credentials, cost of capital,
  and management capability requirements

Capital Allocation Framework:
- Upstream: maintained-for-value vs. growth capex; break-even oil price
  for each project in the portfolio; carbon cost embedded in project economics
- Clean energy investments: evaluate the risk-adjusted returns of
  renewable energy, hydrogen, carbon capture (CCS/CCUS), and EV charging
  vs. the E&P business; why many oil majors earn lower returns on
  renewable investments (competition from financial capital, lower leverage)
- Share buybacks vs. investment: at what oil price should the company
  prioritize buybacks vs. investment? FCF breakeven analysis

Stranded Asset Risk:
- Carbon budget analysis: how much of the company's proved reserves
  are "burnable" under a 1.5°C vs. 2.0°C scenario?
- Asset impairment risk: which long-dated, high-cost assets (oil sands,
  deep-water, LNG) face stranded asset risk under various carbon pricing
  scenarios?
- M&A implications: divest high-carbon-intensity assets to reduce
  transition risk and improve ESG credentials; identify buyers (NOCs,
  smaller E&P companies)

Clean Energy M&A Strategy:
- Build vs. buy in clean energy: developing greenfield renewables vs.
  acquiring an established renewable developer/IPP; make vs. buy analysis
- Hydrogen strategy: green H2 (electrolysis) vs. blue H2 (SMR + CCS);
  which markets have the near-term demand (industrial decarbonization)
- Carbon offset and CCUS investment: cost per tonne of CCS; credibility
  for corporate net-zero claims

Format as a Goldman Sachs Energy Transition Strategy Report with:
  • Business Model Strategic Options Matrix (TSR vs. ESG vs. Risk)
  • Capital Allocation Framework (Oil Price Scenarios)
  • Stranded Asset Risk Quantification
  • Clean Energy M&A Target Screening Criteria
  • 5-Year Financial Projection Under Each Strategy

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | OIL EQUIVALENT PRODUCTION (BOE/DAY) | RESERVE LIFE |
            CURRENT CLEAN ENERGY % OF CAPEX | NET-ZERO COMMITMENT |
            SHAREHOLDER RETURN HISTORY | PRIMARY STRATEGIC DILEMMA]

PROMPT 94 — The Macquarie Green Infrastructure & Battery Storage Investment Analyzer
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

- Revenue streams for BESS: (1) Capacity payments ($/MW-year from ISOs:

- Smart grid and digital grid: advanced metering infrastructure (AMI),

Clean Energy Infrastructure Platform Strategy:

PROMPT 95 — The Morgan Stanley REIT Equity Research Initiation Framework
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Equity Research Analyst at Morgan Stanley covering Real
Estate Investment Trusts (REITs) across industrial, residential, office,
retail, healthcare, and data center sectors.

I need a comprehensive REIT equity research framework covering REIT-specific
financial metrics, NAV-based valuation, and sector rotation strategy.

Analyze:

REIT-Specific Financial Metrics:
- Funds from Operations (FFO): net income + real estate depreciation +
  gains/losses on property sales; the primary earnings metric for REITs
- Adjusted FFO (AFFO): FFO − recurring capex (maintenance capex and
  tenant improvement allowances); the best proxy for REIT dividend
  sustainability
- Net Asset Value (NAV): estimate the market value of all real estate
  assets (Cap Rate applied to NOI) − net debt − other liabilities
  = NAV per share; compare to current share price (premium or discount
  to NAV)
- Dividend yield and payout ratio: REITs must distribute 90%+ of taxable
  income; AFFO payout ratio (target < 85% for a healthy coverage ratio)
- Same-property NOI growth: organic growth in NOI from the existing
  portfolio (rent growth − vacancy change − operating cost change)

Sector-Specific Analysis:
- Industrial/Logistics REITs (Prologis, EastGroup): rent mark-to-market
  potential (in-place rents vs. market rents as a % gap — a key NAV
  catalyst), e-commerce penetration tailwind, development pipeline yield
- Data Center REITs (Equinix, Digital Realty): hyperscaler demand for
  colocation, interconnection revenue density ($/sqft), AI demand
  acceleration, power capacity utilization, and development yields
- Residential REITs (AvalonBay, Camden): supply pipeline analysis by
  market, rent-to-own affordability gap, demographic demand drivers
- Office REITs: work-from-home secular headwind, flight-to-quality
  (trophy assets vs. commodity), return-to-office metrics by market

REIT Valuation:
- Cap rate analysis: current going-in cap rates vs. historical range vs.
  10-year Treasury spread (the "spread to risk-free" as a valuation anchor)
- Premium/discount to NAV: justified premium (high-quality platform with
  development pipeline and access to low-cost capital) vs. discount
  (sectors facing secular headwinds)
- Dividend yield vs. AFFO yield vs. bond yield: relative value analysis

Format as a Morgan Stanley REIT Initiation Framework with:
  • REIT Metrics Dashboard (FFO, AFFO, NAV, Payout Ratio)
  • NAV Build-Up by Property Segment
  • Cap Rate vs. Treasury Spread Chart
  • Sector Attractiveness Matrix (Secular Tailwind × Valuation)
  • Price Target Derivation (Blend of NAV and AFFO Multiple)

My inputs: [REIT NAME | TICKER | SECTOR | PORTFOLIO SIZE ($ AND PROPERTIES) |
            CURRENT STOCK PRICE | ESTIMATED NAV/SHARE | DIVIDEND YIELD |
            PRIMARY INVESTMENT THESIS]

PROMPT 96 — The Wells Fargo Commercial Real Estate (CRE) Loan Underwriting & CMBS Analysis
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Wells Fargo's Commercial Real Estate Finance group,
which is both a major balance-sheet CRE lender and a leading CMBS (Commercial
Mortgage-Backed Securities) originator.

I need a comprehensive CRE loan underwriting and CMBS investment analysis
framework covering property-level underwriting, loan structuring, and
CMBS bond analysis.

Design the framework:

CRE Property Underwriting:
- Income approach: Effective Gross Income (EGI) = potential gross income
  × (1 − vacancy) − collection loss → Net Operating Income (NOI) after
  operating expenses → Debt Service Coverage Ratio (DSCR) = NOI / annual
  debt service
- Underwriting conservatism: difference between in-place rent (actual)
  vs. underwritten rent (market); difference between actual vacancy vs.
  underwritten vacancy (lender applies more stress)
- Property types underwriting nuances:
  Multifamily: unit mix, market rents, vacancy, concessions, rent
    control risk
  Office: lease rollover schedule, WALT, tenant credit quality,
    work-from-home risk
  Retail: anchor tenant quality, sales per square foot, lease structure
    (percentage rent provisions)
  Industrial: rental rate growth, lease term, functional obsolescence
  Hospitality: RevPAR, ADR, occupancy; brand flag value
- LTV constraint: maximum LTV for each property type (multifamily: 75–80%;
  office: 55–65%; hospitality: 55–60%)
- Stressed Debt Yield = NOI ÷ Loan Amount (lenders increasingly use
  this vs. LTV as it is cap-rate agnostic); minimum 8–9% stressed
  debt yield threshold

CMBS Structure & Analysis:
- CMBS trust structure: pool of CRE loans → SPV → tranching into rated
  bonds (AAA → AA → A → BBB → BB → B → unrated B-piece)
- Sequential pay vs. pro-rata: how principal payments flow through the
  capital structure; accelerated pay in stress scenarios
- CMBS bond analysis: spread to swaps by rating, DSCR, LTV, and debt
  yield at issue; how to identify CMBS bonds trading cheap vs. fair value
- B-piece buyer analysis: the most junior bond tranche; how B-piece
  buyers influence loan underwriting through the negotiation process

Format as a Wells Fargo CRE Underwriting Framework with:
  • Property Underwriting Template (NOI → DSCR → LTV → Loan Amount)
  • Loan Terms by Property Type Table
  • CMBS Capital Structure Diagram
  • CMBS Bond Relative Value Analysis
  • Stress Test (NOI -20% → DSCR impact and debt yield test)

My inputs: [PROPERTY TYPE | LOCATION | PURCHASE PRICE | IN-PLACE NOI |
            VACANCY RATE | WALT | PROPOSED LOAN AMOUNT AND LTV |
            LOAN PURPOSE (ACQUISITION / REFINANCING / CONSTRUCTION)]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 97 — The JLL Real Estate Advisory: Corporate Real Estate Strategy & Portfolio Optimization
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at JLL (Jones Lang LaSalle) Corporate Solutions —
advising Fortune 500 corporations on optimizing their corporate real estate
(CRE) portfolios to reduce occupancy costs, improve workforce productivity,
and achieve sustainability commitments.

I need a comprehensive corporate real estate strategy covering portfolio

Design the strategy:

Corporate Real Estate Portfolio Analysis:
- Portfolio inventory: total footprint (square feet or square meters),

  (WALT), annual occupancy cost (rent, service charges, rates, utilities)

  occupancy rate vs. leased space; identify under-utilized locations
  where significant space can be surrendered

  locations for targeted savings
- Lease liability exposure: IFRS 16 / ASC 842 balance sheet impact;
  total undiscounted lease obligations by year; weighted average discount
  rate applied

Portfolio Rationalization Strategy:
- Consolidation opportunities: reduce the number of locations by
  consolidating nearby facilities; estimate savings per consolidation
- Lease renegotiation: for leases with 2–3 years remaining, negotiate
  early renewal in exchange for improved terms (rent reduction, capex
  from landlord, break option); current soft office market creates
  tenant leverage

  assignment; market liquidity for subleases in target cities
- Sale-leaseback: monetize owned properties while retaining operational
  control; estimate the net present value benefit of freeing up capital

Workplace Strategy (Post-COVID):
- Hybrid work design: space allocation based on actual utilization

- ESG & sustainability: energy performance certificate (EPC) ratings;

Format as a JLL Corporate Real Estate Strategy Report with:

PROMPT 98 — The HSBC Trade Finance & Supply Chain Finance Structuring Advisor
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at HSBC's Global Trade and Receivables Finance
(GTRF) group — the world's largest trade finance bank — who structures
complex trade finance and supply chain finance (SCF) solutions for
multinational corporations.

I need a comprehensive trade finance and supply chain finance strategy
for a multinational company with complex cross-border trade flows.

Design the program:

Trade Finance Product Suite:
- Letters of Credit (LC): sight LC vs. usance LC; how LC mitigates
  payment risk for both buyer and seller; standby LC for performance
  guarantees; LC discounting to accelerate seller's cash collection
- Documentary Collections: D/P (documents against payment) vs. D/A
  (documents against acceptance); when appropriate vs. LC; risk profile
- Bank Guarantees & SBLC: performance bonds, advance payment guarantees,
  bid bonds; how to structure guarantees to minimize balance sheet impact
  for the buyer
- Trade Credit Insurance: single-buyer vs. whole-turnover policies;
  ECA (Export Credit Agency) support for emerging market trade;
  EXIM Bank / Euler Hermes / Atradius coverage structures

Supply Chain Finance (SCF) Program Design:
- Reverse factoring (buyer-led SCF): buyer approves invoices → supplier
  gets early payment at buyer's credit rating cost → buyer extends DPO;
  the "win-win-win" for buyer (extended DPO), supplier (early cash),
  and bank (low-risk assets)
- Dynamic discounting: buyer funds early payment from own cash (no bank);
  supplier offers discount for early payment; buyer earns return on cash
- Receivables finance: seller-led; accelerate cash collection from AR
  without taking on debt (true sale off-balance-sheet if structured correctly)
- SCF program sizing: calculate the potential DPO extension value for
  the buyer and early payment benefit for top-50 suppliers

Working Capital & Trade Finance Integration:
- Integrated working capital optimization: combine SCF (extend DPO),
  AR finance (accelerate DSO), and inventory finance → total CCC reduction
  and net cash release
- FX risk in trade finance: how to embed FX hedging into trade finance
  structures; SWIFT FX auto-hedge for cross-border payments

Format as an HSBC Trade Finance Strategy Memo with:
  • Trade Product Selection Matrix (by trade flow type and risk level)
  • SCF Program Design (Supplier Benefit vs. Buyer DPO Extension Table)
  • Working Capital Release Calculation
  • ECA Support Opportunity Assessment
  • Implementation Roadmap (Pilot → Rollout → Optimization)

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | INDUSTRY | ANNUAL TRADE VOLUMES BY GEOGRAPHY |
            CURRENT DPO / DSO | TOP SUPPLIER MARKETS | KEY TRADE RISK CONCERNS |
            TARGET WORKING CAPITAL IMPROVEMENT]

PROMPT 99 — The Barclays Structured Credit & CLO Origination Advisor
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Barclays' Structured Credit and Securitization
group who originates, structures, and distributes Collateralized Loan
Obligations (CLOs), ABS (Asset-Backed Securities), and bespoke structured
credit products.

I need a comprehensive CLO structure analysis and structured credit
investment framework.

Analyze:

CLO Structure & Economics:
- CLO mechanics: a CLO is a securitization vehicle that issues rated debt
  tranches (AAA → AA → A → BBB → BB → B) backed by a pool of ~150–200
  leveraged loans; the equity tranche captures the spread arbitrage
- CLO arbitrage math: Weighted Average Spread (WAS) earned on loan
  portfolio − Weighted Average Cost of Funding (WACF) across all debt
  tranches = gross arbitrage; after management fees and losses → equity IRR
- CLO manager selection: track record of par-building, trading expertise,
  credit avoidance, and rating upgrade history; management fee structure
  (typically 0.40–0.50% senior + 0.20% subordinated)
- CLO lifecycle: warehouse period (build the loan portfolio before pricing),
  closing, reinvestment period (typically 4–5 years), amortization period;
  call/refinance/reset options and their impact on equity IRR

CLO Tranche Investment Analysis:
- AAA CLO notes: the highest-rated floating rate instrument available;
  typical spread over SOFR (90–130bps); key risk: not default risk but
  spread widening / liquidity risk in stress
- Mezzanine CLO tranches (A, BBB, BB): risk/return profile and historical
  default rates by vintage (2006 vs. 2015 vs. 2021 vintages)
- CLO equity: target IRR 12–20% depending on vintage and manager;
  sensitivity to loan defaults, recovery rates, and spread compression;
  the "stack" play (buying equity and junior debt together)

Structured Credit Beyond CLOs:
- CLO ETFs and CLO mutual funds: democratizing access to CLO tranches
- ABS (Auto, Credit Card, Student Loan): relative value vs. CLOs;
  static vs. revolving structures
- Residential Mortgage-Backed Securities (RMBS): agency (GSE-backed,
  credit risk-free) vs. non-agency (private label); prepayment risk
  (CPR modeling) and extension risk in rising rate environments

Format as a Barclays Structured Credit Research Framework with:
  • CLO Arbitrage Math (Sample Deal Economics)
  • CLO Tranche Risk/Return Matrix
  • Vintage Analysis (Historical Default Rates by Tranche and Vintage)
  • CLO Manager Scorecard Template
  • Investment Recommendation by Tranche in Current Market

My inputs: [INVESTOR TYPE | INVESTMENT OBJECTIVE | TARGET YIELD | LIQUIDITY
            REQUIREMENTS | CLO EXPERIENCE LEVEL | CURRENT SPREAD ENVIRONMENT |
            PRIMARY STRUCTURED CREDIT CONCERN]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 100 — The McKinsey Global Institute Financial System Stress Test & Systemic Risk
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at McKinsey's Global Institute (MGI) who advises
central banks, the FSB (Financial Stability Board), and BIS on identifying
and mitigating systemic financial risk — the type of analysis that preceded
and followed the 2008 GFC and 2020 COVID financial shock.

I need a comprehensive systemic financial risk assessment and stress test
of the current financial system, identifying the most dangerous
vulnerabilities and transmission channels.

Analyze:

Systemic Risk Identification:
- Balance sheet vulnerabilities: identify where leverage has accumulated
  most dangerously across: (1) household sector (mortgage debt, consumer
  credit), (2) corporate sector (leveraged loans, high-yield bonds,
  private credit), (3) financial sector (bank leverage, shadow banking
  assets), (4) sovereign sector (debt/GDP levels and sustainability)
- Liquidity mismatches: identify entities that are "borrowing short,
  lending long" at dangerous scale — open-end credit funds with daily
  liquidity but illiquid underlying assets; bank wholesale funding
  dependence; money market fund vulnerability
- Interconnectedness and contagion channels: map the key financial
  linkages — bank-to-bank exposures, bank-to-shadow-bank, asset manager
  herding behavior, and derivative counterparty concentration

Stress Test Scenarios:
- Scenario 1 — Credit Event: a major credit default triggers
  counterparty risk concerns (2008 Lehman-style); which institutions
  are most exposed? What is the contagion pathway?
- Scenario 2 — Liquidity Shock: a sudden withdrawal of liquidity
  from credit markets (2020 March-style); which funding-dependent
  entities face a run? Central bank intervention requirements?
- Scenario 3 — Sovereign Debt Crisis: a major sovereign (EM or
  peripheral Europe) loses market access; fiscal contagion to
  neighboring countries; bank exposure to sovereign bonds
- Scenario 4 — Real Estate Correction: 20–30% decline in CRE or
  residential values; bank loan losses; mortgage servicer stress;
  CMBS market dislocation

Regulatory & Policy Response Framework:
- Macroprudential tools: countercyclical capital buffer (CCyB),
  systemic risk buffer (SRB), LTV and DSCR caps; when to deploy each
- Central bank toolkit: conventional rate cuts, QE, emergency lending
  facilities (BTFP, TALF, PMCCF); sequencing and effectiveness
- Fiscal response: automatic stabilizers vs. discretionary stimulus;
  debt sustainability constraint on fiscal capacity

Portfolio Resilience Assessment:
- For a given investment portfolio: estimate the drawdown under each
  stress scenario; identify the 3 most systemically sensitive holdings;
  design hedges using systemic risk instruments (VIX calls, CDS on
  financial sector, safe haven currency positions)

Format as a McKinsey/FSB Systemic Risk Assessment with:
  • Vulnerability Radar Chart (Household/Corporate/Financial/Sovereign)
  • Contagion Map (described — key transmission channels)
  • 4-Scenario Stress Test Results (Market Impact by Asset Class)
  • Regulatory Policy Response Toolkit
  • Portfolio Resilience Analysis and Recommended Hedges

My inputs: [PORTFOLIO TYPE | ASSET CLASSES HELD | GEOGRAPHIC FOCUS |
            CURRENT MACRO ENVIRONMENT ASSESSMENT | BIGGEST SYSTEMIC
            RISK CONCERN | ACCEPTABLE MAXIMUM DRAWDOWN]

PROMPT 101 — The Goldman Sachs Accretion/Dilution & Exchange Ratio Analysis
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Vice President in Goldman Sachs' M&A Advisory group who builds
accretion/dilution models and exchange ratio analyses for stock-for-stock
mergers and cash/stock combination transactions.

I need a rigorous accretion/dilution analysis and exchange ratio fairness
assessment for a proposed merger of equals or stock-financed acquisition.

Build the full analysis:

Accretion/Dilution Framework:
- Pro-forma EPS model construction:
    • Acquirer standalone EPS (using Street consensus or management case)
    • Target standalone EPS contribution: Target Net Income ×
      (Acquirer shares issued ÷ Target diluted shares outstanding)
    • Synergy after-tax contribution: pre-tax synergies × (1 − tax rate)
    • Financing adjustments: interest income lost on cash used,
      interest expense on new debt, amortization of acquired
      intangibles (PPA step-up × 1/useful life), and transaction costs
    • Pro-forma combined EPS = sum of all above components
    • Accretion/(Dilution) = (Pro-forma EPS − Standalone EPS) ÷
      Standalone EPS × 100 (expressed in % and in cents per share)
- Break-even synergy analysis: the minimum pre-tax synergies required
  for EPS neutrality (Year 1 and Year 2)
- Sensitivity table: accretion/(dilution) at various synergy levels
  (0%, 25%, 50%, 75%, 100% of synergy estimate) × acquisition premium paid
  (0%, 20%, 30%, 40%, 50%)

Exchange Ratio Analysis (for stock-for-stock mergers):
- Exchange ratio calculation: Offer Value per Target Share ÷ Acquirer
  Share Price = Exchange Ratio (target shareholders receive X acquirer
  shares per target share)
- Fixed vs. floating exchange ratio: pros and cons; collar mechanism
  (fixing exchange ratio within a price range to protect both parties)
- Relative contribution analysis: compare each party's contribution to
  the combined entity on Revenue, EBITDA, Earnings, and Book Value;
  does the exchange ratio reflect relative contributions?
- "At-the-market" exchange ratio: the ratio at which the market is
  currently implying the merger (using current share prices); premium
  embedded in the proposed ratio vs. at-market
- Ownership split: pro-forma ownership of acquirer shareholders vs.
  target shareholders in the combined entity; dilution to acquirer
  shareholders

Merger Consequences Analysis:
- Combined company credit profile: leverage ratios, interest coverage,
  credit rating implications (Moody's / S&P likely actions)
- Combined trading multiples: pro-forma EV/EBITDA and P/E vs.
  standalone multiples and sector peers

Format as a Goldman Sachs M&A Model Output with:
  • Accretion/Dilution Summary Table (Year 1 and Year 2)
  • Break-Even Synergy Analysis
  • Sensitivity Matrix (Premium × Synergy → EPS ∆)
  • Exchange Ratio Analysis Table
  • Relative Contribution Table
  • Pro-Forma Credit Metrics

My inputs: [ACQUIRER NAME, EPS, SHARES OUTSTANDING, STOCK PRICE |
            TARGET NAME, EPS, SHARES OUTSTANDING, STOCK PRICE |
            PROPOSED PREMIUM % | FORM OF CONSIDERATION (CASH/STOCK/MIXED) |
            ESTIMATED PRE-TAX SYNERGIES | DEAL FINANCING DETAILS]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 102 — The Morgan Stanley Leveraged Recapitalization & Balance Sheet Restructuring
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director in Morgan Stanley's Corporate Finance group who
advises investment-grade and sub-investment-grade companies on optimal capital
structure, leveraged recapitalizations, and balance sheet restructuring to
maximize shareholder value.

I need a comprehensive capital structure optimization analysis and
leveraged recapitalization assessment.

Deliver:

Optimal Capital Structure Theory vs. Practice:
- Modigliani-Miller with taxes: the tax shield benefit of debt
  (Interest × Tax Rate × Duration); why the optimal capital structure
  is not 100% debt (distress costs, agency costs, loss of financial
  flexibility)
- Trade-off theory: quantify the marginal benefit of each additional
  $1B of debt (tax shield) vs. the marginal cost (increased probability
  of financial distress × distress cost estimate)
- Current vs. optimal leverage: compare the company's Debt/EBITDA to
  the theoretical optimum; quantify the WACC reduction from moving to
  the optimal structure; translate WACC reduction into implied value
  creation per share

Leveraged Recapitalization Analysis:
- Recapitalization options: (1) Special dividend + debt issuance,
  (2) Large-scale accelerated share repurchase (ASR) funded with
  debt, (3) Leveraged recapitalization (raise debt, return cash to
  shareholders while remaining public), (4) Going-private LBO
- For the recommended option, model:
    • New capital structure: current debt + new debt issuance
    • New debt/EBITDA leverage ratio and credit rating implication
    • Special dividend or buyback per share: total cash returned ÷
      diluted shares outstanding
    • EPS accretion from reduced share count (buyback benefit) minus
      EPS dilution from higher interest expense (net EPS impact)
    • Coverage ratios post-recap: Interest Coverage, Fixed Charge
      Coverage; minimum liquidity reserve maintained
- Break-even analysis: what EBITDA decline causes net leverage to
  breach the target maximum (e.g., 3.0× covenant)?

Shareholder Value Creation Framework:
- Value transfer: leverage recap transfers value from debtholders
  (through higher risk) to equity holders — only appropriate when
  the company has predictable FCF and underlevered balance sheet
- Rating agency management: how to communicate the recap to Moody's
  and S&P to minimize downgrade risk; the "financial policy framework"
  that rating agencies want to see

Format as a Morgan Stanley Capital Structure Advisory Memo with:
  • Current vs. Optimal Capital Structure Analysis
  • WACC Optimization Curve (Leverage × WACC → Value per Share)
  • Leveraged Recap Mechanics Table (Sources, Uses, New Capital Structure)
  • EPS Accretion/Dilution from Recap
  • Credit Rating Sensitivity Analysis

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | INDUSTRY | LTM EBITDA | CURRENT NET DEBT |
            CURRENT DEBT/EBITDA | CURRENT SHARE PRICE | SHARES OUTSTANDING |
            FCF CONVERSION RATE | TARGET CAPITAL STRUCTURE RATIONALE]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 103 — The Evercore Cross-Border Merger of Equals (MOE) Structuring
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at Evercore advising the Board of a company engaged
in a cross-border Merger of Equals — the most complex form of M&A transaction
requiring delicate governance, tax, and regulatory navigation.

I need a comprehensive Merger of Equals structuring analysis covering
governance, tax efficiency, domicile selection, and integration design.

Structure the MOE:

MOE Governance Architecture:
- Board composition: 50/50 board representation risks deadlock; how to
  design a governance framework that ensures effective decision-making —
  tie-breaking mechanisms, committee composition, independent directors
- CEO selection: dual-CEO arrangement (always fails eventually) vs.
  single CEO from Day 1 (requires one party to "lose"); CEO selection
  process best practices and typical timeline
- Domicile selection for the combined entity: evaluate UK, Netherlands,
  Ireland, US, and Singapore on: effective tax rate, listing requirements,
  shareholder rights, M&A defense laws, and talent location
- Listing venue: dual-listed company (DLC) structure vs. single
  primary listing with secondary listing; index inclusion implications
  for both shareholder bases

Tax Structure for Cross-Border MOE:
- Tax-free reorganization requirements for US party: Section 368
  continuity of interest, business purpose, and continuity of
  business enterprise tests
- UK stamp duty on share consideration; EU financial transaction tax
  implications; withholding tax on dividends from combined entity
- Optimal holdco structure: NewCo incorporated in tax-efficient
  jurisdiction that can efficiently distribute dividends to both
  sets of shareholders
- IP holding: where to locate combined entity's IP for lowest
  effective tax rate

Regulatory Complexity:
- Multi-jurisdictional antitrust: HSR, EU, UK CMA, plus any sector-
  specific regulators (financial services, defense, telecoms)
- National security review: CFIUS (US) / NSIA (UK) / FIRB (Australia)
  applicability; foreign ownership restrictions in regulated industries
- Employee works council requirements (Germany, Netherlands, France):
  information and consultation obligations that can add 3–6 months

Integration Planning for MOE:
- "Merger of equals" brand management: how to signal true parity to
  employees and markets; name decision (new brand vs. combining names)
- Headquarters location: symbolic importance; talent implications;
  tax considerations for each option
- Cultural integration: two equally powerful cultures — the greatest
  failure risk in MOEs; cultural due diligence methodology

Format as an Evercore MOE Structuring Memorandum with:
  • Governance Architecture Options (Pros/Cons Matrix)
  • Domicile Selection Scorecard
  • Tax Structure Analysis
  • Regulatory Timeline & Risk Assessment
  • MOE Success Factor Checklist (10 factors that predict MOE success)

PROMPT 104 — The Apollo Dividend Recapitalization & PE Return Acceleration Analysis
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Apollo Global Management who evaluates dividend
recapitalization opportunities at PE-backed portfolio companies to accelerate
LP distributions and improve fund-level IRR without exiting the investment.

I need a comprehensive dividend recapitalization analysis for a PE-backed
portfolio company with significant enterprise value appreciation and
debt paydown since close.

Analyze:

Dividend Recap Rationale & Market Timing:
- IRR acceleration mechanics: demonstrate mathematically why returning
  capital early dramatically improves IRR — show the IRR impact of
  receiving a $100M distribution in Year 2 vs. Year 5 at exit
- J-curve mitigation: how dividend recaps help PE funds demonstrate
  early DPI (Distributed to Paid-In) to LPs who increasingly demand
  cash returns vs. paper gains
- Optimal timing: leverage market conditions (credit spread levels,
  covenant terms, lender appetite) — when is the market most receptive
  to levered dividend recap transactions?
- Rating agency and lender considerations: what leverage ratio triggers
  lender consent requirements? How to structure the recap to avoid
  requiring lender approval

Recap Sizing Analysis:
- Maximum debt capacity: existing debt + new debt < maximum sustainable
  leverage (typically 5.5–6.5× EBITDA for quality businesses)
- DSCR maintenance: post-recap interest coverage must remain above
  covenant minimum; model DSCR at Base and Stress EBITDA scenarios
- Net leverage ratio post-recap: acquirer's comfort with leverage
  relative to expected hold period and exit multiple
- Available cash to sponsor: total new debt raised − transaction costs
  (OID, arrangement fees, legal) − cash to company for operations
  = distributable cash

Return Analysis:
- IRR impact: model the fund-level IRR with and without the dividend
  recap across Base / Bull / Bear exit scenarios; show the IRR
  improvement from recap in percentage points
- MOIC impact: recap does not improve MOIC (multiple of invested capital)
  unless the reinvested capital earns a higher return than the
  original investment — explain this nuance
- Risk to exit valuation: does adding leverage increase the probability
  of a distressed exit? Quantify the increased downside risk vs.
  IRR benefit

Debt Instrument Selection for Recap:
- Leveraged loan add-on vs. new bond issuance vs. second lien debt:
  compare on cost, speed, flexibility, and covenant impact
- Debt capacity analysis: incremental first-lien capacity vs. requiring
  a new second lien or subordinated layer

Format as an Apollo Dividend Recap Investment Committee Analysis with:
  • IRR Acceleration Table (Recap vs. No Recap across exit scenarios)
  • Recap Sizing Model (Debt Capacity → Available Distribution)
  • Post-Recap Credit Metrics & Covenant Headroom
  • Risk/Reward Assessment
  • Recommended Recap Size and Instrument

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | INDUSTRY | LTM EBITDA | CURRENT NET DEBT |
            CURRENT ENTERPRISE VALUE | ENTRY EQUITY INVESTED |
            YEARS SINCE CLOSE | TARGET DISTRIBUTION AMOUNT]

PROMPT 106 — The Advent International Carve-Out LBO & Corporate Orphan Finder
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Advent International — a specialist in
corporate carve-outs, which are divisions or subsidiaries being sold by
large corporates that are often "corporate orphans" receiving insufficient
management attention and capital.

I need a comprehensive carve-out LBO investment thesis and value creation
framework for a division being separated from a large corporate parent.

Build the thesis:

Corporate Orphan Identification:
- Why carve-outs create PE value: the division is underinvested (parent
  allocated capex to core business), under-managed (career risk for
  talented managers in a large corporate), strategically misaligned
  (the parent's growth narrative doesn't include this business),
  and potentially undervalued (corporate conglomerate discount)
- Screening for corporate orphans: identify Fortune 500 companies
  with business units that: (a) represent < 10% of parent revenue,
  (b) are in non-core industries vs. parent strategy, (c) have publicly
  disclosed restructuring or portfolio review intentions
- Seller motivation analysis: why is the corporate selling now?
  Strategic refocus, balance sheet repair, activist pressure, or
  CEO change — each implies different seller urgency and price flexibility

Carve-Out-Specific Due Diligence:
- "Orphan business" risks: dissynergies from separation (IT, HR, finance
  infrastructure that must be rebuilt); management team adequacy (often
  the best people left for the parent); revenue at-risk if parent was
  a customer or if brand association is important
- Standalone cost base: fully-loaded standalone P&L after adding all
  necessary functions; run-rate EBITDA with standalone cost adjustments
- TSA dependency: how long will the business rely on parent IT, payroll,
  and other services? TSA exit risk and cost
- Customer concentration: does the parent company represent > 20% of
  revenue? What are the contract terms for that relationship post-sale?

Value Creation Post-Carve-Out:
- Management liberation: new incentive plan aligns management with PE
  sponsor; targets and KPIs that were impossible to set within a large
  corporate context
- Capital allocation freedom: invest in growth capex that the parent
  withheld; R&D reinvestment that drives product differentiation
- Commercial focus: dedicated sales force and marketing; pricing
  optimization that was constrained by parent's enterprise agreements
- Operational excellence: implement lean/continuous improvement
  methodologies; right-size the cost structure for a standalone business

LBO Returns:
- Adjusted EBITDA (standalone) as the LBO entry basis
- Value creation attribution: how much of the return comes from
  (a) EBITDA growth, (b) multiple re-rating (carve-out discount to
  pure-play exit multiple), and (c) leverage paydown

Format as an Advent International Carve-Out Investment Memo with:
  • Corporate Orphan Screening Scorecard
  • Standalone P&L Recast (Parent Reported → Standalone Adjusted EBITDA)
  • Carve-Out Risk Register
  • Value Creation Roadmap (Management, Capital, Commercial)
  • LBO Returns (showing carve-out multiple discount capture)

            PARENT'S STRATEGIC RATIONALE FOR SALE | PRIMARY CARVE-OUT RISK]

PROMPT 107 — The Brevan Howard Global Macro Rates Trading Strategy
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Portfolio Manager at Brevan Howard — one of the world's

and fixed income trading strategies.

Design the strategy:

Global Rates Macro Framework:
- Yield curve decomposition: separate the nominal yield into:
  (1) Real rate (inflation-adjusted expected short rate path),

  component is currently mispriced

  BoE, BoJ, SNB, RBA, BoC), map: current policy rate, expected terminal
  rate, and rate cut/hike timing vs. market pricing; identify where
  market pricing is most wrong
- Cross-market yield spread analysis: identify historically anomalous
  yield spreads (e.g., US 10yr vs. German 10yr Bund spread at extremes;
  UK gilt spread vs. Eurozone; Japan yield curve control unwind risk)

  - Stop loss and profit target (in bps of yield or price points)
  - Position sizing: how much duration risk (DV01) is appropriate
  - Carry and roll: is the trade positive or negative carry while waiting
    for the thesis to play out?
  - Key catalyst: specific data or policy event that will move the trade

Yield Curve Trade Construction:
- Curve steepener vs. flattener: when to implement 2s10s vs. 2s30s
  trades; the role of the yield curve shape in economic forecasting
- Butterfly trades: 2s5s10s or 5s10s30s butterflies to express views
  on the belly of the curve without taking outright duration risk
- Basis trades: on-the-run vs. off-the-run Treasury basis; cash/futures
  basis and repo rate dynamics

Risk Management for Rates Books:
- DV01 (Dollar Value of 01): aggregate and position-level limits;
  how to report and monitor duration risk
- Convexity risk in bond portfolios; managing convexity in
  mortgage-backed securities (negative convexity)
- Correlation risk: rate volatility contagion from equities
  (the stock-bond correlation regime in inflationary environments)

Format as a Brevan Howard Rates Strategy Memo with:
  • Central Bank Divergence Matrix
  • 5 Specific Rate Trade Ideas (Entry/Stop/Target/Carry/Catalyst)

  • Risk Attribution by Trade

PROMPT 108 — The Man Group / AHL Systematic FX Strategy & Currency Overlay
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Portfolio Manager at Man AHL — the systematic trading arm
of Man Group, managing $50B+ in systematic strategies — designing rules-
based currency trading and FX overlay strategies.

I need a comprehensive systematic FX strategy covering carry, momentum,
value, and macro factor models for currency markets.

Design the FX strategy:

Currency Factor Framework:
1. Carry Strategy:
   - Construction: long high-yield currencies (AUD, NZD, MXN, BRL, ZAR,
     IDR), short low-yield currencies (JPY, CHF, EUR) weighted by
     interest rate differential
   - Risk management: carry crashes (when risk-off triggers sudden
     unwinding — 2008, 2015, 2020); use volatility-adjusted position
     sizing; implement stop losses based on VIX or credit spread levels
   - Expected Sharpe Ratio: carry typically delivers 0.5–0.8 Sharpe
     before hedging, materially lower after accounting for crash risk

2. Momentum Strategy:
   - Construction: rank G10 + EM currencies by 1-month, 3-month,
     and 12-month return; long top quintile, short bottom quintile
   - Trend following in FX: how trend filters (200-day MA crossover,
     ATR breakout) have historically performed in currency markets
   - Correlation: currency momentum is largely uncorrelated to equity
     momentum — diversification benefit

3. Value/PPP Strategy:
   - Purchasing Power Parity (PPP) deviation: currencies more than
     2 standard deviations cheap vs. PPP are long candidates;
     mean reversion is slow (3–5 years) so appropriate for long-term
     overlays not tactical trading
   - Real Effective Exchange Rate (REER): similar approach using
     trade-weighted inflation-adjusted exchange rates
   - Limitation: value in FX has weak short-term predictive power;
     combine with carry and momentum for better timing

4. Macro FX Framework:
   - Growth differentials: stronger GDP growth → currency appreciation;
     model the relationship between PMI differentials and 3-month FX returns
   - Current account dynamics: persistent current account surplus countries
     tend to see currency appreciation over 1–3 year horizons
   - Commodity currency models: AUD/NZD/CAD/NOK as commodity proxies;
     model against relevant commodity price indices

Portfolio Construction:
- Multi-factor combination: how to weight carry, momentum, value, and macro
  for optimal Sharpe; IC-weighted combination vs. equal weight
- Execution: impact costs in G10 vs. EM currencies; best execution windows;
  FX fixing vs. market order

Format as a Man AHL Systematic FX Strategy Specification with:
  • Factor Construction Methodology for Each of 4 Factors
  • Backtested Performance Metrics (Sharpe, Max Drawdown, Hit Rate)
  • Multi-Factor Portfolio Weights
  • Risk Management Rules (Position Limits, Stop Losses, Crash Protection)
  • Current Trade Book (Top 5 Long, Top 5 Short Positions)

My inputs: [PORTFOLIO SIZE | G10 ONLY OR G10+EM | LEVERAGE LIMIT |
            HEDGING OBJECTIVE OR ALPHA OBJECTIVE | BENCHMARK CURRENCY |
            BIGGEST FX RISK CONCERN IN UNDERLYING PORTFOLIO]

PROMPT 109 — The Glencore Commodity Trading Strategy & Physical-Financial Arbitrage
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Trading Director at Glencore's Trading & Marketing division —
the world's most sophisticated commodity trading operation — responsible for
identifying and capturing commodity trading opportunities across energy,
metals, and agricultural commodities.

I need a comprehensive commodity trading strategy covering physical-financial
arbitrage, term structure analysis, and commodity risk management.

Design the trading strategy:

Commodity Market Structure Analysis:
- Term structure (forward curve) analysis:
    • Backwardation: spot price > futures price; indicates physical
      tightness and strong carry for long positions (convenience yield)
    • Contango: futures price > spot price; indicates physical surplus;
      creates carry costs for long positions; enables cash-and-carry
      arbitrage for storage-capable traders
    • Key insight: how Glencore profits from contango through physical
      storage arbitrage — buy spot, store commodity, sell forward at a
      premium that exceeds storage cost
- Physical vs. financial basis: the spread between physical commodity
  price (specific grade, specific location) and the futures contract
  price (standardized grade, delivery point); how to capture basis
  convergence profits
- Seasonal patterns by commodity: crude oil (hurricane season, driving
  season, winter heating), natural gas (injection vs. withdrawal season),
  agricultural commodities (harvest cycles)

Specific Trading Opportunities:
For each of the 3 major commodity groups, identify 1 specific trade:

Energy (Crude, NatGas, Refined Products):
  - Crack spread trading: refinery margin capture (crude oil vs.
    gasoline + diesel output); how to construct the 3-2-1 crack spread
    and when it is historically attractive

Metals (Copper, Aluminum, Zinc, Nickel):
  - LME cash-3month spread: backwardation/contango indicator of
    near-term physical tightness; how to trade the spread between
    LME spot and 3-month futures
  - Geographic arbitrage: LME (London) vs. SHFE (Shanghai) copper
    price spread; import/export parity calculations; how physical
    traders capture geographic price differentials

Agricultural Commodities:
  - Crush spread (soybeans): soybeans → soybean meal + soybean oil;
    the "board crush" as a proxy for crushing margins; seasonal patterns
  - Weather-driven trading: how ENSO (El Niño/La Niña) affects Brazilian
    corn/soy production; satellite-based crop monitoring signals

Risk Management for Commodity Trading:
- VaR and CVaR for commodity books; historical simulation approach
- Position limits by commodity and tenor
- Stress testing: commodity price -40% scenario (2020 oil crash replay)

Format as a Commodity Trading Strategy Report with:
  • Term Structure Analysis Dashboard (Backwardation/Contango Indicators)
  • 3 Specific Trade Ideas (Commodity | Entry | Stop | Target | Carry)
  • Physical-Financial Arbitrage Mechanics (Worked Example)
  • Seasonal Calendar by Commodity
  • Risk Management Framework

My inputs: [COMMODITY FOCUS (ENERGY/METALS/AGRI/MULTI) | TRADING APPROACH
            (PHYSICAL/FINANCIAL/BOTH) | RISK CAPITAL | POSITION LIMITS |
            CURRENT MARKET VIEW | PRIMARY RISK CONCERN]

PROMPT 112 — The Weil Gotshal Corporate Governance Crisis Management Advisor

- When to form a Special Committee: accounting fraud allegations, CEO

PROMPT 113 — The a16z / Ribbit Capital Neobank & Digital Banking Investment Thesis
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

venture and growth equity firm — who has invested in Robinhood, Revolut,

Build the investment thesis:

Neobank Business Model Assessment:
- Customer acquisition strategy: freemium model (free account, paid
  premium tier); referral/viral mechanics; brand/performance marketing
  mix; CAC by channel and trend (improving or deteriorating?)
- Primary monetization streams: rank by profitability and sustainability
  (1) Interchange revenue (Durbin-exempt if < $10B assets: 1.5–2.0%),
  (2) Subscription/premium tier (monthly fee for premium features),
  (3) Lending (BNPL, earned wage access, personal loans, credit card),
  (4) Investment services (stock trading, crypto, robo-advisory),
  (5) Cross-border / FX (margin on international transfers),
  (6) B2B2C / embedded finance (white-label banking platform)
- Revenue per user (ARPU) analysis: ARPU by product/service; identify
  the cross-sell sequence that maximizes LTV; engagement depth (MAU,
  DAU, products per user)
- Unit economics at scale: model the per-customer P&A at 1M, 5M,
  and 20M customers; when does the business achieve per-unit profitability?

Regulatory & Charter Strategy:
- Bank charter decision: de novo bank charter (high bar, high benefit —
  access to Fed Master Account, deposit insurance, ability to lend
  from own balance sheet) vs. bank partner model (faster, but revenue
  sharing and operational dependency on partner bank)
- Compliance cost structure: KYC/AML cost per new customer; fraud
  loss rate and management; the regulatory cost curve as the neobank
  grows from startup to regulated bank
- Durbin Amendment threshold: approaching $10B in assets triggers
  Durbin, cutting interchange revenue by ~50% — model the ARPU impact

Competitive Positioning:
- vs. Traditional banks: lower cost structure (no branches), better
  UX/design, but limited product depth; where is the neobank winning?
- vs. Other neobanks: identify the 3 most direct competitors; what is
  the sustainable differentiation (specific segment focus, product
  innovation, network effects)?
- The "winner-take-most" question: is digital banking winner-take-all,
  or can multiple neobanks coexist? Historical evidence from developed
  and emerging markets

Format as a Ribbit Capital Neobank Investment Memo with:
  • Business Model Quality Scorecard
  • Unit Economics Model (CAC → LTV by cohort vintage)
  • Revenue Mix Evolution (Year 1 → Year 5)
  • Regulatory Strategy Recommendation
  • Fund Return Analysis at Proposed Valuation

            PRIMARY REVENUE STREAMS | BANK CHARTER STATUS |
            PROPOSED VALUATION | ROUND SIZE]

PROMPT 115 — The JPMorgan Blockchain & Digital Assets Institutional Framework

  genuine value only when: (1) multiple parties need to share data,

  immutability is required, (4) the parties don't trust each other

    (3) Trade finance on blockchain: Marco Polo, Contour (HSBC/ING);

        Templeton on-chain money market fund; liquidity implications

  projects fail because a database would be cheaper and faster

- Crypto market segmentation: L1 blockchains (Bitcoin, Ethereum),

PROMPT 117 — The Oliver Wyman Industrial Manufacturing Operational Excellence Program
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at Oliver Wyman's Manufacturing & Operations practice

- OEE (Overall Equipment Effectiveness) baseline: OEE = Availability

  COQ as % of revenue; benchmark vs. industry best practice (COQ < 2%
  of revenue for leading manufacturers)

  inventory turns vs. sector benchmarks; root cause of delivery failures
  (planning, capacity, supplier performance)
- Productivity benchmarking: revenue per manufacturing employee vs.
  sector median; units produced per shift; labor efficiency ratio

Lean Manufacturing Transformation:
- Value Stream Mapping (VSM): map the current state for the top
  3 product families; identify waste (MUDA) under the 8 categories
  (overproduction, waiting, transport, over-processing, inventory,
  motion, defects, talent waste); design the future state VSM
- Kaizen event design: structured rapid improvement events for
  prioritized waste elimination; typical payback < 3 months
- TPM (Total Productive Maintenance): autonomous maintenance, planned
  maintenance, and early equipment management — reducing unplanned
  downtime from 15%+ to < 5%
- Production scheduling: pull vs. push scheduling; takt time analysis;
  one-piece flow vs. batch production economics

Supply Chain & Procurement Excellence:

  sourcing for critical components; total cost of ownership (TCO) model

Format as an Oliver Wyman Manufacturing Excellence Program with:

PROMPT 118 — The BCG Industrial Decarbonization & Green Steel/Cement Strategy
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at BCG's Industrial Decarbonization practice who
advises hard-to-abate industrial companies (steel, cement, chemicals,

commercially viable.

abate industrial company facing increasing carbon pricing and customer

Design the strategy:

Hard-to-Abate Industry Carbon Profile:
- Why these industries are hard to abate: process emissions (limestone
  calcination in cement, coking coal in blast furnace steel) that cannot
  be eliminated simply by electrification; the technological maturity
  timeline for green alternatives
- Carbon cost exposure: current and projected carbon price (ETS, CBAM
  implications); calculate the total annual carbon cost at €50, €100,
  and €200/tonne CO2 for this specific industrial process; % of EBITDA
  at risk at each price level

  competitive dynamics; importers from non-carbon-priced regions must
  now pay the EU carbon price — quantify the competitive advantage
  for domestic producers with credible decarbonization roadmaps

Decarbonization Technology Pathways:
- Green Steel: (1) Electric Arc Furnace (EAF) using scrap steel
  (already green if powered by renewables, but scrap-constrained),
  (2) Direct Reduced Iron (DRI) using green hydrogen (the "green
  steel" endgame — cost at scale, capex, timeline), (3) CCS retrofit
  on blast furnaces (intermediate solution)
- Green Cement: clinker substitution (SCMs: fly ash, GGBS, calcined
  clay); novel cements (LC3, Calix Flash Calciner); CCS for process
  emissions; timeline and cost to scale

- Cost trajectory: LCOH (Levelized Cost of Green Hydrogen) from
  $5–7/kg today to $1–2/kg by 2030 — how this changes decarbonization
  economics fundamentally

Commercial Strategy for First Movers:
- Premium pricing for green products: quantify the "green premium"
  customers are willing to pay (automotive OEMs, construction companies,
  consumer goods companies with Scope 3 commitments)
- Strategic partnerships: PPA for renewable power, offtake agreements
  for green hydrogen with electrolyzer manufacturers, joint ventures
  with technology providers
- Green capex financing: ETS revenue reinvestment, green bonds, EU
  Just Transition Fund, hydrogen bank support (EU, US IRA hydrogen PTC)

Format as a BCG Industrial Decarbonization Strategy Report with:
  • Carbon Cost Exposure Analysis (Current vs. 2030 vs. 2040)
  • Technology Pathway Comparison (Cost, Timeline, Maturity)
  • Decarbonization Roadmap (Phase 1: Efficiency → Phase 2: Fuel Switch
    → Phase 3: Process Change)
  • Green Premium Commercial Strategy
  • Financing Plan for Green Capex
