PROMPT 119 — The BIS / Federal Reserve Monetary Policy Transmission Research
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Economist at the Bank for International Settlements (BIS) or
Federal Reserve Research Division who publishes working papers on monetary
policy transmission mechanisms and their evolving effectiveness.

I need a rigorous academic-quality analysis of monetary policy transmission
channels and their effectiveness in the current economic environment.

Research the transmission mechanisms:

Monetary Policy Transmission Channels:
1. Interest Rate Channel:
   - How rate changes pass through to: (a) bank lending rates (prime,
     mortgage, auto), (b) corporate bond yields, (c) consumer and
     business borrowing demand; lags in transmission (typically
     12–18 months for full effect on inflation)
   - Interest rate pass-through completeness: has the recent rate
     hiking cycle fully passed through to lending rates? Compare
     pass-through vs. historical cycles

2. Bank Lending Channel:
   - Balance sheet channel: as rates rise, bank capital deteriorates
     (unrealized losses on bond portfolios); reduced bank capital →
     reduced lending capacity; Silicon Valley Bank collapse as a
     case study in this mechanism
   - Credit availability: tightening of lending standards (Senior
     Loan Officer Opinion Survey — SLOOS data); sectoral credit
     allocation shifts

3. Asset Price / Wealth Channel:
   - How rate hikes reduce equity and property valuations (via
     discount rate effect); the wealth effect on consumption
     (particularly for top-income households who own most assets)
   - Reverse wealth effect: when rising house prices increase
     consumption vs. when falling prices reduce it

4. Exchange Rate Channel:
   - Interest rate differential → capital flows → currency appreciation
     → import price deflation; quantify the exchange rate contribution
     to inflation reduction in an open economy

5. Expectations Channel:
   - Forward guidance effectiveness: how credibly communicating future
     rate intentions moves long-term rates today; the role of central
     bank credibility (inflation expectations anchoring)
   - Phillips Curve and NAIRU: is the traditional Phillips Curve
     relationship still intact? Evidence from recent cycles

Effectiveness Assessment:
- Is monetary policy tightening working? Scorecard across channels
- Heterogeneous effects: which household and corporate segments are
  most affected? (Variable rate borrowers, overleveraged corporates,
  rate-sensitive industries)
- Limits of monetary policy: supply-side inflation, energy price
  shocks — what monetary policy cannot fix

Format as a BIS Working Paper Structure with:
  • Transmission Channel Effectiveness Matrix
  • Empirical Evidence Summary (Key Studies Cited)
  • Heterogeneous Impact Analysis
  • Policy Effectiveness Scorecard
  • Implications for Current Monetary Policy Stance

My inputs: [GEOGRAPHIC FOCUS (US / EURO AREA / UK / GLOBAL) |
            CURRENT POLICY RATE | INFLATION TARGET VS. CURRENT INFLATION |
            PRIMARY CONCERN ABOUT TRANSMISSION EFFECTIVENESS |
            SPECIFIC CHANNEL TO FOCUS ON]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 120 — The IMF Article IV Consultation & Country Economic Assessment
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior IMF Mission Chief conducting an Article IV Consultation —
the annual economic health check that the IMF performs for all 190 member
countries — which results in a Staff Report with policy recommendations.

I need a comprehensive Article IV-style economic assessment for a specific
country, covering macroeconomic stability, fiscal sustainability, financial
sector health, and structural reform priorities.

Conduct the assessment:

Macroeconomic Performance Assessment:
- GDP growth decomposition: actual vs. potential (output gap); demand
  components (C + I + G + NX); identify whether growth is balanced or
  over-reliant on one driver (e.g., consumption funded by credit)
- Inflation analysis: headline vs. core CPI; decompose into: food,
  energy, services, and goods; inflation expectations anchoring
- Labor market: unemployment rate vs. NAIRU; labor force participation
  trend; real wage growth; productivity growth
- External sector: current account balance (surplus or deficit), its
  sustainability, and financing sources; exchange rate assessment
  (IMF REER and EBA methodology); reserve adequacy

Fiscal Sustainability Assessment:
- Fiscal stance: cyclically adjusted primary balance vs. debt-
  stabilizing primary balance; is fiscal policy procyclical?
- Debt sustainability analysis (DSA): Debt/GDP trajectory under
  base, upside, and stress scenarios; primary balance required for
  debt stabilization; gross financing need (GFN) as % of GDP
- Fiscal risk assessment: contingent liabilities (SOEs, PPPs, sub-
  national governments, state banks); pension system sustainability;
  natural resource revenue dependency

Financial Sector Assessment:
- Banking system health: capital adequacy (Tier 1, NPL ratios),
  profitability (ROA, ROE), and liquidity (LCR, NSFR); systemic banks
  identified; stress test results
- Credit growth: is credit growth outpacing GDP growth? Household
  debt-to-income and corporate debt-to-EBITDA ratios
- Dollarization risk: share of foreign currency loans and deposits;
  currency mismatch in bank balance sheets

Policy Recommendations (IMF Article IV Style):
- Monetary policy recommendation: appropriate policy rate path given
  inflation and growth outlook
- Fiscal policy recommendation: consolidation path; structural
  measures to improve revenue and expenditure quality
- Structural reform priorities: top 3 reforms that would most improve
  medium-term growth potential (business environment, labor market,
  financial sector development)

Format as an IMF Article IV Staff Report Outline with:
  • Macroeconomic Dashboard (Key Indicators vs. Peers)
  • Debt Sustainability Analysis Table
  • Financial Sector Health Scorecard
  • Policy Recommendation Matrix
  • Risk Assessment (External, Domestic, Upside Risks)

My inputs: [COUNTRY NAME | CURRENT GDP AND GROWTH RATE | DEBT/GDP |
            FISCAL DEFICIT | INFLATION RATE | CURRENT ACCOUNT BALANCE |
            CURRENCY REGIME | PRIMARY ECONOMIC CONCERN]

PROMPT 101 — The Goldman Sachs Accretion/Dilution & Exchange Ratio Analysis
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Vice President in Goldman Sachs' M&A Advisory group who builds
accretion/dilution models and exchange ratio analyses for stock-for-stock
mergers and cash/stock combination transactions.

I need a rigorous accretion/dilution analysis and exchange ratio fairness
assessment for a proposed merger of equals or stock-financed acquisition.

Build the full analysis:

Accretion/Dilution Framework:
- Pro-forma EPS model construction:
    • Acquirer standalone EPS (using Street consensus or management case)
    • Target standalone EPS contribution: Target Net Income ×
      (Acquirer shares issued ÷ Target diluted shares outstanding)
    • Synergy after-tax contribution: pre-tax synergies × (1 − tax rate)
    • Financing adjustments: interest income lost on cash used,
      interest expense on new debt, amortization of acquired
      intangibles (PPA step-up × 1/useful life), and transaction costs
    • Pro-forma combined EPS = sum of all above components
    • Accretion/(Dilution) = (Pro-forma EPS − Standalone EPS) ÷
      Standalone EPS × 100 (expressed in % and in cents per share)
- Break-even synergy analysis: the minimum pre-tax synergies required
  for EPS neutrality (Year 1 and Year 2)
- Sensitivity table: accretion/(dilution) at various synergy levels
  (0%, 25%, 50%, 75%, 100% of synergy estimate) × acquisition premium paid
  (0%, 20%, 30%, 40%, 50%)

Exchange Ratio Analysis (for stock-for-stock mergers):
- Exchange ratio calculation: Offer Value per Target Share ÷ Acquirer
  Share Price = Exchange Ratio (target shareholders receive X acquirer
  shares per target share)
- Fixed vs. floating exchange ratio: pros and cons; collar mechanism
  (fixing exchange ratio within a price range to protect both parties)
- Relative contribution analysis: compare each party's contribution to
  the combined entity on Revenue, EBITDA, Earnings, and Book Value;
  does the exchange ratio reflect relative contributions?
- "At-the-market" exchange ratio: the ratio at which the market is
  currently implying the merger (using current share prices); premium
  embedded in the proposed ratio vs. at-market
- Ownership split: pro-forma ownership of acquirer shareholders vs.
  target shareholders in the combined entity; dilution to acquirer
  shareholders

Merger Consequences Analysis:
- Combined company credit profile: leverage ratios, interest coverage,
  credit rating implications (Moody's / S&P likely actions)
- Combined trading multiples: pro-forma EV/EBITDA and P/E vs.
  standalone multiples and sector peers

Format as a Goldman Sachs M&A Model Output with:
  • Accretion/Dilution Summary Table (Year 1 and Year 2)
  • Break-Even Synergy Analysis
  • Sensitivity Matrix (Premium × Synergy → EPS ∆)
  • Exchange Ratio Analysis Table
  • Relative Contribution Table
  • Pro-Forma Credit Metrics

My inputs: [ACQUIRER NAME, EPS, SHARES OUTSTANDING, STOCK PRICE |
            TARGET NAME, EPS, SHARES OUTSTANDING, STOCK PRICE |
            PROPOSED PREMIUM % | FORM OF CONSIDERATION (CASH/STOCK/MIXED) |
            ESTIMATED PRE-TAX SYNERGIES | DEAL FINANCING DETAILS]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 102 — The Morgan Stanley Leveraged Recapitalization & Balance Sheet Restructuring
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director in Morgan Stanley's Corporate Finance group who
advises investment-grade and sub-investment-grade companies on optimal capital
structure, leveraged recapitalizations, and balance sheet restructuring to
maximize shareholder value.

I need a comprehensive capital structure optimization analysis and
leveraged recapitalization assessment.

Deliver:

Optimal Capital Structure Theory vs. Practice:
- Modigliani-Miller with taxes: the tax shield benefit of debt
  (Interest × Tax Rate × Duration); why the optimal capital structure
  is not 100% debt (distress costs, agency costs, loss of financial
  flexibility)
- Trade-off theory: quantify the marginal benefit of each additional
  $1B of debt (tax shield) vs. the marginal cost (increased probability
  of financial distress × distress cost estimate)
- Current vs. optimal leverage: compare the company's Debt/EBITDA to
  the theoretical optimum; quantify the WACC reduction from moving to
  the optimal structure; translate WACC reduction into implied value
  creation per share

Leveraged Recapitalization Analysis:
- Recapitalization options: (1) Special dividend + debt issuance,
  (2) Large-scale accelerated share repurchase (ASR) funded with
  debt, (3) Leveraged recapitalization (raise debt, return cash to
  shareholders while remaining public), (4) Going-private LBO
- For the recommended option, model:
    • New capital structure: current debt + new debt issuance
    • New debt/EBITDA leverage ratio and credit rating implication
    • Special dividend or buyback per share: total cash returned ÷
      diluted shares outstanding
    • EPS accretion from reduced share count (buyback benefit) minus
      EPS dilution from higher interest expense (net EPS impact)
    • Coverage ratios post-recap: Interest Coverage, Fixed Charge
      Coverage; minimum liquidity reserve maintained
- Break-even analysis: what EBITDA decline causes net leverage to
  breach the target maximum (e.g., 3.0× covenant)?

Shareholder Value Creation Framework:
- Value transfer: leverage recap transfers value from debtholders
  (through higher risk) to equity holders — only appropriate when
  the company has predictable FCF and underlevered balance sheet
- Rating agency management: how to communicate the recap to Moody's
  and S&P to minimize downgrade risk; the "financial policy framework"
  that rating agencies want to see

Format as a Morgan Stanley Capital Structure Advisory Memo with:
  • Current vs. Optimal Capital Structure Analysis
  • WACC Optimization Curve (Leverage × WACC → Value per Share)
  • Leveraged Recap Mechanics Table (Sources, Uses, New Capital Structure)
  • EPS Accretion/Dilution from Recap
  • Credit Rating Sensitivity Analysis

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | INDUSTRY | LTM EBITDA | CURRENT NET DEBT |
            CURRENT DEBT/EBITDA | CURRENT SHARE PRICE | SHARES OUTSTANDING |
            FCF CONVERSION RATE | TARGET CAPITAL STRUCTURE RATIONALE]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 103 — The Evercore Cross-Border Merger of Equals (MOE) Structuring
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at Evercore advising the Board of a company engaged
in a cross-border Merger of Equals — the most complex form of M&A transaction
requiring delicate governance, tax, and regulatory navigation.

I need a comprehensive Merger of Equals structuring analysis covering
governance, tax efficiency, domicile selection, and integration design.

Structure the MOE:

MOE Governance Architecture:
- Board composition: 50/50 board representation risks deadlock; how to
  design a governance framework that ensures effective decision-making —
  tie-breaking mechanisms, committee composition, independent directors
- CEO selection: dual-CEO arrangement (always fails eventually) vs.
  single CEO from Day 1 (requires one party to "lose"); CEO selection
  process best practices and typical timeline
- Domicile selection for the combined entity: evaluate UK, Netherlands,
  Ireland, US, and Singapore on: effective tax rate, listing requirements,
  shareholder rights, M&A defense laws, and talent location
- Listing venue: dual-listed company (DLC) structure vs. single
  primary listing with secondary listing; index inclusion implications
  for both shareholder bases

Tax Structure for Cross-Border MOE:
- Tax-free reorganization requirements for US party: Section 368
  continuity of interest, business purpose, and continuity of
  business enterprise tests
- UK stamp duty on share consideration; EU financial transaction tax
  implications; withholding tax on dividends from combined entity
- Optimal holdco structure: NewCo incorporated in tax-efficient
  jurisdiction that can efficiently distribute dividends to both
  sets of shareholders
- IP holding: where to locate combined entity's IP for lowest
  effective tax rate

Regulatory Complexity:
- Multi-jurisdictional antitrust: HSR, EU, UK CMA, plus any sector-
  specific regulators (financial services, defense, telecoms)
- National security review: CFIUS (US) / NSIA (UK) / FIRB (Australia)
  applicability; foreign ownership restrictions in regulated industries
- Employee works council requirements (Germany, Netherlands, France):
  information and consultation obligations that can add 3–6 months

Integration Planning for MOE:
- "Merger of equals" brand management: how to signal true parity to
  employees and markets; name decision (new brand vs. combining names)
- Headquarters location: symbolic importance; talent implications;
  tax considerations for each option
- Cultural integration: two equally powerful cultures — the greatest
  failure risk in MOEs; cultural due diligence methodology

Format as an Evercore MOE Structuring Memorandum with:
  • Governance Architecture Options (Pros/Cons Matrix)
  • Domicile Selection Scorecard
  • Tax Structure Analysis
  • Regulatory Timeline & Risk Assessment
  • MOE Success Factor Checklist (10 factors that predict MOE success)

My inputs: [COMPANY A NAME, COUNTRY, REVENUE, MARKET CAP |
            COMPANY B NAME, COUNTRY, REVENUE, MARKET CAP |
            INDUSTRY | STRATEGIC RATIONALE | PRIMARY CONCERN FOR THE BOARD]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 104 — The Apollo Dividend Recapitalization & PE Return Acceleration Analysis
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Apollo Global Management who evaluates dividend
recapitalization opportunities at PE-backed portfolio companies to accelerate
LP distributions and improve fund-level IRR without exiting the investment.

I need a comprehensive dividend recapitalization analysis for a PE-backed
portfolio company with significant enterprise value appreciation and
debt paydown since close.

Analyze:

Dividend Recap Rationale & Market Timing:
- IRR acceleration mechanics: demonstrate mathematically why returning
  capital early dramatically improves IRR — show the IRR impact of
  receiving a $100M distribution in Year 2 vs. Year 5 at exit
- J-curve mitigation: how dividend recaps help PE funds demonstrate
  early DPI (Distributed to Paid-In) to LPs who increasingly demand
  cash returns vs. paper gains
- Optimal timing: leverage market conditions (credit spread levels,
  covenant terms, lender appetite) — when is the market most receptive
  to levered dividend recap transactions?
- Rating agency and lender considerations: what leverage ratio triggers
  lender consent requirements? How to structure the recap to avoid
  requiring lender approval

Recap Sizing Analysis:
- Maximum debt capacity: existing debt + new debt < maximum sustainable
  leverage (typically 5.5–6.5× EBITDA for quality businesses)
- DSCR maintenance: post-recap interest coverage must remain above
  covenant minimum; model DSCR at Base and Stress EBITDA scenarios
- Net leverage ratio post-recap: acquirer's comfort with leverage
  relative to expected hold period and exit multiple
- Available cash to sponsor: total new debt raised − transaction costs
  (OID, arrangement fees, legal) − cash to company for operations
  = distributable cash

Return Analysis:
- IRR impact: model the fund-level IRR with and without the dividend
  recap across Base / Bull / Bear exit scenarios; show the IRR
  improvement from recap in percentage points
- MOIC impact: recap does not improve MOIC (multiple of invested capital)
  unless the reinvested capital earns a higher return than the
  original investment — explain this nuance
- Risk to exit valuation: does adding leverage increase the probability
  of a distressed exit? Quantify the increased downside risk vs.
  IRR benefit

Debt Instrument Selection for Recap:
- Leveraged loan add-on vs. new bond issuance vs. second lien debt:
  compare on cost, speed, flexibility, and covenant impact
- Debt capacity analysis: incremental first-lien capacity vs. requiring
  a new second lien or subordinated layer

Format as an Apollo Dividend Recap Investment Committee Analysis with:
  • IRR Acceleration Table (Recap vs. No Recap across exit scenarios)
  • Recap Sizing Model (Debt Capacity → Available Distribution)
  • Post-Recap Credit Metrics & Covenant Headroom
  • Risk/Reward Assessment
  • Recommended Recap Size and Instrument

My inputs: [COMPANY NAME | INDUSTRY | LTM EBITDA | CURRENT NET DEBT |
            CURRENT ENTERPRISE VALUE | ENTRY EQUITY INVESTED |
            YEARS SINCE CLOSE | TARGET DISTRIBUTION AMOUNT]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 105 — The Warburg Pincus PE Add-On Acquisition & Platform Build Strategy
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Warburg Pincus who oversees buy-and-build
platform strategies — acquiring a founder-led business as the platform and
systematically executing add-on acquisitions to create a scaled, premium-
multiple business.

I need a comprehensive add-on M&A strategy and financial framework for
a PE-backed platform executing a buy-and-build thesis.

Design the strategy:

Platform Investment Thesis:
- Buy-and-build rationale: why multiple arbitrage is achievable in this
  sector (fragmented market, multiple small players trading at 4–6×
  EBITDA vs. a consolidated platform that exits at 10–14× EBITDA);
  quantify the value creation from arbitrage vs. organic growth
- Target market fragmentation analysis: estimate the number of
  potential acquisition targets; average size ($M EBITDA); geographic
  distribution; typical founder-owned vs. PE-backed mix
- Platform quality criteria: what makes the platform company the
  ideal consolidator? (Operating systems, back-office infrastructure,
  brand/reputation, capital access, management depth)

Add-On Screening & Prioritization Framework:
- Target screening criteria: minimum EBITDA ($M), growth rate,
  customer overlap (complementary vs. competing), geographic fit,
  service/product adjacency, and cultural alignment
- Triage framework: Tier 1 (immediate outreach), Tier 2 (12-month
  pipeline), Tier 3 (opportunistic)
- Proprietary deal sourcing: how to find founder-owned businesses
  before they engage a sell-side advisor (direct outreach, industry
  conference relationships, referral networks)

Add-On Valuation & Integration:
- Purchase price discipline: maximum entry multiple for each add-on
  that still creates value for the combined platform; "walk-away"
  price discipline
- Integration playbook: standardized 100-day integration plan for
  add-ons — finance/reporting conversion (Day 1–30), systems
  migration (Day 31–90), commercial integration (Day 61–180)
- Synergy realization: revenue synergies (cross-sell, geographic
  expansion) and cost synergies (back-office consolidation, procurement);
  realistic realization timeline (faster for cost, slower for revenue)

Portfolio Return Modeling:
- Pro-forma model: platform + 5 add-ons executed over 3 years;
  combined EBITDA, implied multiple arbitrage value, and fund-level
  IRR and MOIC
- Sensitivity: how does the IRR change if add-on multiples are higher
  than expected? If integration costs exceed budget?
- Exit options: strategic sale to a larger PE or corporate, IPO of
  the consolidated platform, continuation fund

Format as a Warburg Pincus Buy-and-Build Strategy Memo with:
  • Multiple Arbitrage Value Creation Quantification
  • Add-On Target Screening Matrix
  • Integration Playbook Summary
  • Pro-Forma Platform Build Model (5 add-ons over 3 years)
  • Fund Return Analysis (Base / Bull / Bear)

My inputs: [PLATFORM COMPANY | INDUSTRY | PLATFORM EBITDA |
            TARGET NUMBER OF ADD-ONS | TYPICAL ADD-ON SIZE |
            AVAILABLE ACQUISITION CAPITAL | HOLD PERIOD | EXIT MULTIPLE THESIS]

PROMPT 106 — The Advent International Carve-Out LBO & Corporate Orphan Finder
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Managing Director at Advent International — a specialist in
corporate carve-outs, which are divisions or subsidiaries being sold by
large corporates that are often "corporate orphans" receiving insufficient
management attention and capital.

I need a comprehensive carve-out LBO investment thesis and value creation
framework for a division being separated from a large corporate parent.

Build the thesis:

Corporate Orphan Identification:
- Why carve-outs create PE value: the division is underinvested (parent
  allocated capex to core business), under-managed (career risk for
  talented managers in a large corporate), strategically misaligned
  (the parent's growth narrative doesn't include this business),
  and potentially undervalued (corporate conglomerate discount)
- Screening for corporate orphans: identify Fortune 500 companies
  with business units that: (a) represent < 10% of parent revenue,
  (b) are in non-core industries vs. parent strategy, (c) have publicly
  disclosed restructuring or portfolio review intentions
- Seller motivation analysis: why is the corporate selling now?
  Strategic refocus, balance sheet repair, activist pressure, or
  CEO change — each implies different seller urgency and price flexibility

Carve-Out-Specific Due Diligence:
- "Orphan business" risks: dissynergies from separation (IT, HR, finance
  infrastructure that must be rebuilt); management team adequacy (often
  the best people left for the parent); revenue at-risk if parent was
  a customer or if brand association is important
- Standalone cost base: fully-loaded standalone P&L after adding all
  necessary functions; run-rate EBITDA with standalone cost adjustments
- TSA dependency: how long will the business rely on parent IT, payroll,
  and other services? TSA exit risk and cost
- Customer concentration: does the parent company represent > 20% of
  revenue? What are the contract terms for that relationship post-sale?

Value Creation Post-Carve-Out:
- Management liberation: new incentive plan aligns management with PE
  sponsor; targets and KPIs that were impossible to set within a large
  corporate context
- Capital allocation freedom: invest in growth capex that the parent
  withheld; R&D reinvestment that drives product differentiation
- Commercial focus: dedicated sales force and marketing; pricing
  optimization that was constrained by parent's enterprise agreements
- Operational excellence: implement lean/continuous improvement
  methodologies; right-size the cost structure for a standalone business

LBO Returns:
- Adjusted EBITDA (standalone) as the LBO entry basis
- Value creation attribution: how much of the return comes from
  (a) EBITDA growth, (b) multiple re-rating (carve-out discount to
  pure-play exit multiple), and (c) leverage paydown

Format as an Advent International Carve-Out Investment Memo with:
  • Corporate Orphan Screening Scorecard
  • Standalone P&L Recast (Parent Reported → Standalone Adjusted EBITDA)
  • Carve-Out Risk Register
  • Value Creation Roadmap (Management, Capital, Commercial)
  • LBO Returns (showing carve-out multiple discount capture)

My inputs: [PARENT COMPANY | DIVISION BEING CARVED OUT | INDUSTRY |
            REPORTED DIVISIONAL REVENUE & EBITDA | PROPOSED ACQUISITION PRICE |
            PARENT'S STRATEGIC RATIONALE FOR SALE | PRIMARY CARVE-OUT RISK]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 107 — The Brevan Howard Global Macro Rates Trading Strategy
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Portfolio Manager at Brevan Howard — one of the world's
most respected macro hedge funds — specializing in global interest rate
and fixed income trading strategies.

I need a comprehensive global rates macro trading strategy covering
yield curve positioning, relative value trades, and central bank
divergence plays.

Design the strategy:

Global Rates Macro Framework:
- Yield curve decomposition: separate the nominal yield into:
  (1) Real rate (inflation-adjusted expected short rate path),
  (2) Inflation expectations (breakeven inflation), and
  (3) Term premium (compensation for duration risk) — identify which
  component is currently mispriced
- Central bank divergence map: for each major central bank (Fed, ECB,
  BoE, BoJ, SNB, RBA, BoC), map: current policy rate, expected terminal
  rate, and rate cut/hike timing vs. market pricing; identify where
  market pricing is most wrong
- Cross-market yield spread analysis: identify historically anomalous
  yield spreads (e.g., US 10yr vs. German 10yr Bund spread at extremes;
  UK gilt spread vs. Eurozone; Japan yield curve control unwind risk)

Specific Rate Trades (5 high-conviction ideas):
For each trade, specify:
  - Instrument: specific bond futures, IRS, OIS, or swaptions
  - Direction: long rates (buy bonds/receive fixed) vs. short rates
    (sell bonds/pay fixed)
  - Entry level and rationale (fundamental mispricing)
  - Stop loss and profit target (in bps of yield or price points)
  - Position sizing: how much duration risk (DV01) is appropriate
  - Carry and roll: is the trade positive or negative carry while waiting
    for the thesis to play out?
  - Key catalyst: specific data or policy event that will move the trade

Yield Curve Trade Construction:
- Curve steepener vs. flattener: when to implement 2s10s vs. 2s30s
  trades; the role of the yield curve shape in economic forecasting
- Butterfly trades: 2s5s10s or 5s10s30s butterflies to express views
  on the belly of the curve without taking outright duration risk
- Basis trades: on-the-run vs. off-the-run Treasury basis; cash/futures
  basis and repo rate dynamics

Risk Management for Rates Books:
- DV01 (Dollar Value of 01): aggregate and position-level limits;
  how to report and monitor duration risk
- Convexity risk in bond portfolios; managing convexity in
  mortgage-backed securities (negative convexity)
- Correlation risk: rate volatility contagion from equities
  (the stock-bond correlation regime in inflationary environments)

Format as a Brevan Howard Rates Strategy Memo with:
  • Central Bank Divergence Matrix
  • 5 Specific Rate Trade Ideas (Entry/Stop/Target/Carry/Catalyst)
  • Yield Curve Positioning Summary
  • Risk Attribution by Trade
  • Portfolio DV01 and Scenario P&L Analysis

My inputs: [CURRENT PORTFOLIO RATE EXPOSURE | GEOGRAPHIC FOCUS |
            DV01 BUDGET | LEVERAGE CONSTRAINTS | KEY MACRO VIEW |
            BIGGEST RATES RISK IN THE PORTFOLIO]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 108 — The Man Group / AHL Systematic FX Strategy & Currency Overlay
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Portfolio Manager at Man AHL — the systematic trading arm
of Man Group, managing $50B+ in systematic strategies — designing rules-
based currency trading and FX overlay strategies.

I need a comprehensive systematic FX strategy covering carry, momentum,
value, and macro factor models for currency markets.

Design the FX strategy:

Currency Factor Framework:
1. Carry Strategy:
   - Construction: long high-yield currencies (AUD, NZD, MXN, BRL, ZAR,
     IDR), short low-yield currencies (JPY, CHF, EUR) weighted by
     interest rate differential
   - Risk management: carry crashes (when risk-off triggers sudden
     unwinding — 2008, 2015, 2020); use volatility-adjusted position
     sizing; implement stop losses based on VIX or credit spread levels
   - Expected Sharpe Ratio: carry typically delivers 0.5–0.8 Sharpe
     before hedging, materially lower after accounting for crash risk

2. Momentum Strategy:
   - Construction: rank G10 + EM currencies by 1-month, 3-month,
     and 12-month return; long top quintile, short bottom quintile
   - Trend following in FX: how trend filters (200-day MA crossover,
     ATR breakout) have historically performed in currency markets
   - Correlation: currency momentum is largely uncorrelated to equity
     momentum — diversification benefit

3. Value/PPP Strategy:
   - Purchasing Power Parity (PPP) deviation: currencies more than
     2 standard deviations cheap vs. PPP are long candidates;
     mean reversion is slow (3–5 years) so appropriate for long-term
     overlays not tactical trading
   - Real Effective Exchange Rate (REER): similar approach using
     trade-weighted inflation-adjusted exchange rates
   - Limitation: value in FX has weak short-term predictive power;
     combine with carry and momentum for better timing

4. Macro FX Framework:
   - Growth differentials: stronger GDP growth → currency appreciation;
     model the relationship between PMI differentials and 3-month FX returns
   - Current account dynamics: persistent current account surplus countries
     tend to see currency appreciation over 1–3 year horizons
   - Commodity currency models: AUD/NZD/CAD/NOK as commodity proxies;
     model against relevant commodity price indices

Portfolio Construction:
- Multi-factor combination: how to weight carry, momentum, value, and macro
  for optimal Sharpe; IC-weighted combination vs. equal weight
- Execution: impact costs in G10 vs. EM currencies; best execution windows;
  FX fixing vs. market order

Format as a Man AHL Systematic FX Strategy Specification with:
  • Factor Construction Methodology for Each of 4 Factors
  • Backtested Performance Metrics (Sharpe, Max Drawdown, Hit Rate)
  • Multi-Factor Portfolio Weights
  • Risk Management Rules (Position Limits, Stop Losses, Crash Protection)
  • Current Trade Book (Top 5 Long, Top 5 Short Positions)

My inputs: [PORTFOLIO SIZE | G10 ONLY OR G10+EM | LEVERAGE LIMIT |
            HEDGING OBJECTIVE OR ALPHA OBJECTIVE | BENCHMARK CURRENCY |
            BIGGEST FX RISK CONCERN IN UNDERLYING PORTFOLIO]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 109 — The Glencore Commodity Trading Strategy & Physical-Financial Arbitrage
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Trading Director at Glencore's Trading & Marketing division —
the world's most sophisticated commodity trading operation — responsible for
identifying and capturing commodity trading opportunities across energy,
metals, and agricultural commodities.

I need a comprehensive commodity trading strategy covering physical-financial
arbitrage, term structure analysis, and commodity risk management.

Design the trading strategy:

Commodity Market Structure Analysis:
- Term structure (forward curve) analysis:
    • Backwardation: spot price > futures price; indicates physical
      tightness and strong carry for long positions (convenience yield)
    • Contango: futures price > spot price; indicates physical surplus;
      creates carry costs for long positions; enables cash-and-carry
      arbitrage for storage-capable traders
    • Key insight: how Glencore profits from contango through physical
      storage arbitrage — buy spot, store commodity, sell forward at a
      premium that exceeds storage cost
- Physical vs. financial basis: the spread between physical commodity
  price (specific grade, specific location) and the futures contract
  price (standardized grade, delivery point); how to capture basis
  convergence profits
- Seasonal patterns by commodity: crude oil (hurricane season, driving
  season, winter heating), natural gas (injection vs. withdrawal season),
  agricultural commodities (harvest cycles)

Specific Trading Opportunities:
For each of the 3 major commodity groups, identify 1 specific trade:

Energy (Crude, NatGas, Refined Products):
  - Crack spread trading: refinery margin capture (crude oil vs.
    gasoline + diesel output); how to construct the 3-2-1 crack spread
    and when it is historically attractive

Metals (Copper, Aluminum, Zinc, Nickel):
  - LME cash-3month spread: backwardation/contango indicator of
    near-term physical tightness; how to trade the spread between
    LME spot and 3-month futures
  - Geographic arbitrage: LME (London) vs. SHFE (Shanghai) copper
    price spread; import/export parity calculations; how physical
    traders capture geographic price differentials

Agricultural Commodities:
  - Crush spread (soybeans): soybeans → soybean meal + soybean oil;
    the "board crush" as a proxy for crushing margins; seasonal patterns
  - Weather-driven trading: how ENSO (El Niño/La Niña) affects Brazilian
    corn/soy production; satellite-based crop monitoring signals

Risk Management for Commodity Trading:
- VaR and CVaR for commodity books; historical simulation approach
- Position limits by commodity and tenor
- Stress testing: commodity price -40% scenario (2020 oil crash replay)

Format as a Commodity Trading Strategy Report with:
  • Term Structure Analysis Dashboard (Backwardation/Contango Indicators)
  • 3 Specific Trade Ideas (Commodity | Entry | Stop | Target | Carry)
  • Physical-Financial Arbitrage Mechanics (Worked Example)
  • Seasonal Calendar by Commodity
  • Risk Management Framework

My inputs: [COMMODITY FOCUS (ENERGY/METALS/AGRI/MULTI) | TRADING APPROACH
            (PHYSICAL/FINANCIAL/BOTH) | RISK CAPITAL | POSITION LIMITS |
            CURRENT MARKET VIEW | PRIMARY RISK CONCERN]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 110 — The Spencer Stuart Board Effectiveness & CEO Succession Advisor
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at Spencer Stuart's Board and CEO Practice — the
most prestigious executive search and board advisory firm — who advises
Nomination Committees on board effectiveness, director recruitment, and
CEO succession planning.

I need a comprehensive board effectiveness assessment and CEO succession
planning framework for a public company Board.

Design the framework:

Board Composition & Effectiveness Assessment:
- Skills matrix audit: map current directors' expertise against the
  skills the company needs over the next 5 years (technology, ESG,
  international, financial, sector-specific, regulatory); identify gaps
- Independence assessment: classify each director by independence status;
  review for "over-boarding" (too many board seats) and "overtenure"
  (> 9 years on the same board — loss of independence risk); ISS and
  Glass Lewis governance standards

  expectations; disclosure requirements (Nasdaq Rule 5605(f), SEC rules)

  composition — quality of debate, challenge culture, information flow,

CEO Succession Planning:
- Proactive vs. reactive succession: why every board should maintain
  a CEO succession plan even when the current CEO has years left;
  the "3 names in an envelope" standard
- Internal vs. external CEO: data on performance of internal vs.
  external CEO hires; when each is more appropriate; how to develop
  internal successors without triggering retention issues in those not

- CEO profile definition: translate the company's 5-year strategy into

- Assessment process: how to rigorously assess internal candidates;
  external benchmarking; psychological assessment and reference checking
- Transition planning: announcing succession without disruption;
  onboarding the new CEO; the outgoing CEO's role post-transition

CEO Compensation Philosophy:
- Pay-for-performance alignment: short-term incentive (annual bonus)
  and long-term incentive (equity) design; metric selection rationale;
  performance period length; vesting schedule
- Say-on-pay risk: ISS and Glass Lewis pay program analysis;
  common triggers for "Against" recommendations; how to design
  a compensation program that passes vote thresholds (typically > 90%)

Format as a Spencer Stuart Board Advisory Report with:
  • Director Skills Matrix (Current State vs. Required Future Skills)
  • Board Composition Scorecard
  • CEO Succession Candidate Assessment Framework
  • CEO Compensation Design Principles
  • 12-Month Board Improvement Roadmap

            COMPANY STRATEGY FOR NEXT 5 YEARS | CEO TENURE |
            RECENT PROXY VOTE RESULTS | PRIMARY GOVERNANCE CONCERN]

PROMPT 111 — The ISS / Glass Lewis Proxy Advisory & Shareholder Engagement Strategy
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Director at a leading proxy advisory firm (ISS or Glass Lewis)
or as an Investor Relations professional who deeply understands proxy advisory
methodology and how to proactively manage the annual meeting and engage
institutional shareholders.

I need a comprehensive proxy season preparation strategy covering voting
agenda risk, proxy advisory engagement, and institutional investor outreach.

Design the proxy strategy:

Proxy Season Risk Assessment:
- Agenda item risk classification: for each voting item, estimate the
  probability of a material "Against" or "Withhold" vote:
    • Director elections: independence, overboarding, audit committee
      financial expertise, overtenure, diversity deficit, responsiveness
      to prior-year vote outcomes
    • Executive compensation (Say-on-Pay): pay-for-performance alignment
      using ISS GAAP EPS test and relative TSR test; potential concerns
      (supplemental metrics, quantum, peer group selection, special
      awards, single-trigger change-of-control)
    • Shareholder proposals: ESG (climate disclosure, political spending,
      board diversity), governance (declassify board, majority voting,
      independent chair), and executive compensation proposals
    • Equity plan approval: ISS Shareholder Value Transfer (SVT) test;
      burn rate concerns; plan terms (evergreen provision, liberal change-
      of-control, repricing without shareholder approval)

ISS & Glass Lewis Methodology:
- Explain ISS's quantitative pay-for-performance test: how ISS compares
  CEO pay vs. peers and compares pay and TSR rank — the most common
  trigger for "Against" SoP recommendations
- Glass Lewis's distinct focus areas vs. ISS: board oversight, proxy
  statement disclosure quality, and governance structure preferences
- When the two disagree and what that means for the vote outcome

Institutional Investor Engagement Program:
- Pre-proxy season outreach calendar: when to meet with governance
  teams of top-25 institutional holders (September–December for
  January proxy filings); agenda and format for governance discussions
- "Big Three" engagement (BlackRock, Vanguard, State Street): their
  stewardship priorities, voting guidelines, and how their governance
  team differs from their investment team
- Responsive disclosure: how to address prior-year vote outcomes in
  the proxy statement; the "responsiveness" standard that ISS requires
- Proxy statement drafting: how to write Compensation Discussion &
  Analysis (CD&A) that tells a compelling pay-for-performance story

Format as a Proxy Season Preparation Playbook with:
  • Proxy Agenda Risk Assessment Table
  • ISS & Glass Lewis Vote Outcome Prediction
  • Institutional Investor Engagement Calendar
  • Compensation Disclosure Checklist
  • Response Plan for Any Item with > 20% "Against" Risk

            KEY CONCERNS FROM PRIOR YEAR VOTE | MAJOR INSTITUTIONAL HOLDERS |
            CEO PAY QUANTUM | ANY SPECIAL EQUITY AWARDS THIS YEAR]

PROMPT 112 — The Weil Gotshal Corporate Governance Crisis Management Advisor
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at Weil, Gotshal & Manges advising the Special
Committee of an independent board on governance crisis management — covering
executive misconduct investigations, securities litigation, and regulatory
enforcement response.

I need a comprehensive corporate governance crisis management framework
for a board facing a significant governance failure.

Design the crisis response:

Special Committee Formation & Independence:
- When to form a Special Committee: accounting fraud allegations, CEO
  misconduct, related-party transaction dispute, conflicted M&A process,
  or securities class action — the circumstances that require independent
  investigation insulated from full board
- Committee composition: independence requirements (no relationships
  with the subject of the investigation); relevant expertise (legal,
  financial, industry); number of directors (typically 3)
- Engaging independent advisors: selecting counsel and financial advisors
  who have no prior relationship with the company or the subject of
  the investigation; importance of privilege protection

Internal Investigation Framework:
- Scope definition: define the investigation mandate clearly but not
  so narrowly that it misses the full extent of the issue
- Document preservation: litigation hold notice — what to preserve,
  how to communicate to employees, the consequences of document
  destruction (spoliation sanctions)
- Witness interviews: sequence (peripheral witnesses first, key
  actors last); recording protocols; subject employee rights
- Digital forensics: email and chat message review; forensic imaging
  of devices; AI-assisted document review for speed and cost efficiency
- Investigation timeline: typical 3–12 months depending on scope;
  interim findings reporting cadence to the Special Committee

Regulatory & Legal Response:
- SEC Wells Notice response: timeline, process, and how to make
  a compelling case against enforcement action
- DOJ Cooperation Credit: how corporations earn cooperation credit
  under the FCPA, securities fraud, and antitrust guidelines;
  Yates Memo implications for individual accountability
- Clawback policy enforcement: SOX/Dodd-Frank clawback requirements;
  how boards enforce clawback of executive compensation

Stakeholder Communication:
- Crisis disclosure: when does a governance failure require 8-K
  disclosure? Materiality assessment under SEC rules
- Employee communication: how to maintain morale and retain talent
  during an investigation

PROMPT 113 — The a16z / Ribbit Capital Neobank & Digital Banking Investment Thesis
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

or digital banking startup.

Build the investment thesis:

Neobank Business Model Assessment:
- Customer acquisition strategy: freemium model (free account, paid
  premium tier); referral/viral mechanics; brand/performance marketing
  mix; CAC by channel and trend (improving or deteriorating?)
- Primary monetization streams: rank by profitability and sustainability
  (1) Interchange revenue (Durbin-exempt if < $10B assets: 1.5–2.0%),
  (2) Subscription/premium tier (monthly fee for premium features),
  (3) Lending (BNPL, earned wage access, personal loans, credit card),
  (4) Investment services (stock trading, crypto, robo-advisory),
  (5) Cross-border / FX (margin on international transfers),

  sharing and operational dependency on partner bank)
- Compliance cost structure: KYC/AML cost per new customer; fraud
  loss rate and management; the regulatory cost curve as the neobank
  grows from startup to regulated bank
- Durbin Amendment threshold: approaching $10B in assets triggers
  Durbin, cutting interchange revenue by ~50% — model the ARPU impact

Competitive Positioning:
- vs. Traditional banks: lower cost structure (no branches), better
  UX/design, but limited product depth; where is the neobank winning?
- vs. Other neobanks: identify the 3 most direct competitors; what is
  the sustainable differentiation (specific segment focus, product

- The "winner-take-most" question: is digital banking winner-take-all,
  or can multiple neobanks coexist? Historical evidence from developed
  and emerging markets

Format as a Ribbit Capital Neobank Investment Memo with:

  • Fund Return Analysis at Proposed Valuation

PROMPT 114 — The McKinsey Embedded Finance & Banking-as-a-Service (BaaS) Strategy
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Design the strategy:

For Banks Building a BaaS Platform:
- BaaS business model: the bank provides regulated financial infrastructure
  (deposit accounts, payments, card issuing, lending) via API to non-bank
  distributors (fintechs, SaaS companies, marketplaces) who serve the
  end customer
- Revenue model: per-transaction fees, monthly platform fees, interchange
  revenue sharing, spread income on deposits held — model the revenue
  per embedded customer vs. direct bank customer
- Technology architecture: core banking system API-ification; middleware
  layer for partner onboarding and compliance; real-time compliance
  monitoring at scale
- Partner selection and risk management: compliance liability of the bank

  acquire a BaaS-native bank (Piermont, Column, Thread), or partner
  with a BaaS middleware provider (Unit, Treasury Prime, Synctera)

For Non-Financial Companies Embedding Finance:
- Embedded finance opportunity sizing: for a SaaS company, marketplace,
  or platform — how much additional ARPU can be unlocked through
  embedding payments, lending, insurance, or investment products?
  Use benchmarks: Toast (payments + lending), Shopify (payments + lending
  + banking), Uber (Uber Money), Amazon (Buy with Prime, lending)

- Strategic rationale beyond revenue: data capture (financial transaction

- Regulatory considerations: when does embedding finance make the
  platform a "financial institution" subject to regulation?

Format as a McKinsey Embedded Finance Strategy Report with:

PROMPT 115 — The JPMorgan Blockchain & Digital Assets Institutional Framework
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

covering real use cases, crypto market analysis, and risk management.

Design the institutional framework:

Blockchain Use Cases — Value vs. Hype:
- Framework for evaluating blockchain use cases: a blockchain adds
  genuine value only when: (1) multiple parties need to share data,
  (2) there is no trusted central intermediary, (3) transparency or
  immutability is required, (4) the parties don't trust each other
  — apply this test to proposed blockchain use cases
- High-value institutional use cases:
    (1) Tokenized securities: faster settlement (T+0 vs. T+2), 24/7
        trading, fractional ownership; current state of Euroclear,
        DTCC, JPM Coin, Broadridge DLR initiatives
    (2) Cross-border payments: SWIFT vs. RippleNet vs. JPM Coin vs.
        stablecoins for wholesale bank settlement; speed and cost
        comparison
    (3) Trade finance on blockchain: Marco Polo, Contour (HSBC/ING);
        smart contract automation of LC issuance and document verification
    (4) Tokenized Funds and Private Markets: Blackrock BUIDL, Franklin
        Templeton on-chain money market fund; liquidity implications
- Low-value use cases (save your time): most enterprise blockchain
  projects fail because a database would be cheaper and faster

Digital Assets Market Structure:
- Crypto market segmentation: L1 blockchains (Bitcoin, Ethereum),
  DeFi protocols, stablecoins (algorithmic vs. fiat-backed), NFTs,
  CBDCs (Central Bank Digital Currencies)
- Institutional grade custody: Coinbase Custody, Fidelity Digital
  Assets, BitGo — segregated cold storage, insurance coverage, SOC 2
  compliance requirements
- Spot crypto ETFs: post-approval market structure; how institutional
  allocation has evolved with ETF access

Institutional Risk Framework:
- Market risk: crypto volatility (Bitcoin 80%+ peak-to-trough
  drawdowns); position sizing for crypto in institutional portfolios
  (typically 1–5% for adventurous allocators)
- Counterparty risk: exchange failure (FTX); on-chain self-custody vs.
  custodian; smart contract risk
- Regulatory risk: MiCA (EU), SAB 121 accounting treatment,
  FASB crypto fair value accounting update; how regulatory clarity
  is evolving

Format as a JPMorgan Institutional Digital Assets Framework with:

  • Regulatory Landscape Map

PROMPT 117 — The Oliver Wyman Industrial Manufacturing Operational Excellence Program
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at Oliver Wyman's Manufacturing & Operations practice
who has led operational excellence and lean manufacturing transformations
at automotive OEMs, industrial equipment companies, and process manufacturers.

program to improve margins, quality, and throughput.

Design the program:

Manufacturing Performance Diagnostic:
- OEE (Overall Equipment Effectiveness) baseline: OEE = Availability
  × Performance × Quality; benchmark vs. world-class (85% for discrete
  manufacturing); identify the top 3 OEE loss categories (unplanned
  downtime, speed loss, quality defects)
- Cost of Quality analysis: Prevention costs, Appraisal costs, Internal
  failure (scrap, rework), External failure (warranty, returns) — total
  COQ as % of revenue; benchmark vs. industry best practice (COQ < 2%
  of revenue for leading manufacturers)
- Supply chain performance: on-time delivery (OTD), lead time, and
  inventory turns vs. sector benchmarks; root cause of delivery failures
  (planning, capacity, supplier performance)
- Productivity benchmarking: revenue per manufacturing employee vs.
  sector median; units produced per shift; labor efficiency ratio

Lean Manufacturing Transformation:
- Value Stream Mapping (VSM): map the current state for the top
  3 product families; identify waste (MUDA) under the 8 categories
  (overproduction, waiting, transport, over-processing, inventory,
  motion, defects, talent waste); design the future state VSM
- Kaizen event design: structured rapid improvement events for
  prioritized waste elimination; typical payback < 3 months
- TPM (Total Productive Maintenance): autonomous maintenance, planned
  maintenance, and early equipment management — reducing unplanned
  downtime from 15%+ to < 5%
- Production scheduling: pull vs. push scheduling; takt time analysis;
  one-piece flow vs. batch production economics

Supply Chain & Procurement Excellence:
- Make vs. buy optimization: reassess which components to manufacture
  internally vs. source externally based on core competency and TCO
- Strategic sourcing: RFQ process, supplier rationalization, dual
  sourcing for critical components; total cost of ownership (TCO) model
  including quality, logistics, and risk costs
- Supplier development program: Tier 1 supplier performance management
  KPIs; supplier OEE improvement program; early supplier involvement
  in new product development

Format as an Oliver Wyman Manufacturing Excellence Program with:
  • OEE & COQ Diagnostic Dashboard

PROMPT 118 — The BCG Industrial Decarbonization & Green Steel/Cement Strategy
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Partner at BCG's Industrial Decarbonization practice who
advises hard-to-abate industrial companies (steel, cement, chemicals,
aluminum) on net-zero transition strategies that are both credible and
commercially viable.

abate industrial company facing increasing carbon pricing and customer
sustainability demands.

Design the strategy:

Hard-to-Abate Industry Carbon Profile:
- Why these industries are hard to abate: process emissions (limestone
  calcination in cement, coking coal in blast furnace steel) that cannot
  be eliminated simply by electrification; the technological maturity
  timeline for green alternatives
- Carbon cost exposure: current and projected carbon price (ETS, CBAM
  implications); calculate the total annual carbon cost at €50, €100,
  and €200/tonne CO2 for this specific industrial process; % of EBITDA
  at risk at each price level
- EU Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM): how CBAM changes
  competitive dynamics; importers from non-carbon-priced regions must
  now pay the EU carbon price — quantify the competitive advantage
  for domestic producers with credible decarbonization roadmaps

Decarbonization Technology Pathways:
- Green Steel: (1) Electric Arc Furnace (EAF) using scrap steel
  (already green if powered by renewables, but scrap-constrained),
  (2) Direct Reduced Iron (DRI) using green hydrogen (the "green
  steel" endgame — cost at scale, capex, timeline), (3) CCS retrofit
  on blast furnaces (intermediate solution)
- Green Cement: clinker substitution (SCMs: fly ash, GGBS, calcined
  clay); novel cements (LC3, Calix Flash Calciner); CCS for process
  emissions; timeline and cost to scale
- Green Chemicals: electrolysis-based green hydrogen replacing
  steam methane reforming; bio-based feedstocks; circular economy
  (plastic waste as chemical feedstock)
- Cost trajectory: LCOH (Levelized Cost of Green Hydrogen) from
  $5–7/kg today to $1–2/kg by 2030 — how this changes decarbonization
  economics fundamentally

Commercial Strategy for First Movers:
- Premium pricing for green products: quantify the "green premium"
  customers are willing to pay (automotive OEMs, construction companies,
  consumer goods companies with Scope 3 commitments)
- Strategic partnerships: PPA for renewable power, offtake agreements
  for green hydrogen with electrolyzer manufacturers, joint ventures
  with technology providers
- Green capex financing: ETS revenue reinvestment, green bonds, EU
  Just Transition Fund, hydrogen bank support (EU, US IRA hydrogen PTC)

Format as a BCG Industrial Decarbonization Strategy Report with:
  • Carbon Cost Exposure Analysis (Current vs. 2030 vs. 2040)
  • Technology Pathway Comparison (Cost, Timeline, Maturity)
  • Decarbonization Roadmap (Phase 1: Efficiency → Phase 2: Fuel Switch
    → Phase 3: Process Change)
  • Green Premium Commercial Strategy
  • Financing Plan for Green Capex

PROMPT 119 — The BIS / Federal Reserve Monetary Policy Transmission Research
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior Economist at the Bank for International Settlements (BIS) or
Federal Reserve Research Division who publishes working papers on monetary
policy transmission mechanisms and their evolving effectiveness.

I need a rigorous academic-quality analysis of monetary policy transmission
channels and their effectiveness in the current economic environment.

Research the transmission mechanisms:

Monetary Policy Transmission Channels:
1. Interest Rate Channel:
   - How rate changes pass through to: (a) bank lending rates (prime,
     mortgage, auto), (b) corporate bond yields, (c) consumer and
     business borrowing demand; lags in transmission (typically
     12–18 months for full effect on inflation)
   - Interest rate pass-through completeness: has the recent rate
     hiking cycle fully passed through to lending rates? Compare
     pass-through vs. historical cycles

2. Bank Lending Channel:
   - Balance sheet channel: as rates rise, bank capital deteriorates
     (unrealized losses on bond portfolios); reduced bank capital →
     reduced lending capacity; Silicon Valley Bank collapse as a
     case study in this mechanism
   - Credit availability: tightening of lending standards (Senior
     Loan Officer Opinion Survey — SLOOS data); sectoral credit
     allocation shifts

3. Asset Price / Wealth Channel:
   - How rate hikes reduce equity and property valuations (via
     discount rate effect); the wealth effect on consumption
     (particularly for top-income households who own most assets)
   - Reverse wealth effect: when rising house prices increase
     consumption vs. when falling prices reduce it

4. Exchange Rate Channel:
   - Interest rate differential → capital flows → currency appreciation
     → import price deflation; quantify the exchange rate contribution
     to inflation reduction in an open economy

5. Expectations Channel:
   - Forward guidance effectiveness: how credibly communicating future
     rate intentions moves long-term rates today; the role of central
     bank credibility (inflation expectations anchoring)
   - Phillips Curve and NAIRU: is the traditional Phillips Curve
     relationship still intact? Evidence from recent cycles

Effectiveness Assessment:
- Is monetary policy tightening working? Scorecard across channels
- Heterogeneous effects: which household and corporate segments are
  most affected? (Variable rate borrowers, overleveraged corporates,
  rate-sensitive industries)
- Limits of monetary policy: supply-side inflation, energy price
  shocks — what monetary policy cannot fix

Format as a BIS Working Paper Structure with:
  • Transmission Channel Effectiveness Matrix
  • Empirical Evidence Summary (Key Studies Cited)
  • Heterogeneous Impact Analysis
  • Policy Effectiveness Scorecard
  • Implications for Current Monetary Policy Stance

My inputs: [GEOGRAPHIC FOCUS (US / EURO AREA / UK / GLOBAL) |
            CURRENT POLICY RATE | INFLATION TARGET VS. CURRENT INFLATION |
            PRIMARY CONCERN ABOUT TRANSMISSION EFFECTIVENESS |
            SPECIFIC CHANNEL TO FOCUS ON]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 120 — The IMF Article IV Consultation & Country Economic Assessment
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Act as a Senior IMF Mission Chief conducting an Article IV Consultation —
the annual economic health check that the IMF performs for all 190 member
countries — which results in a Staff Report with policy recommendations.

I need a comprehensive Article IV-style economic assessment for a specific
country, covering macroeconomic stability, fiscal sustainability, financial
sector health, and structural reform priorities.

Conduct the assessment:

Macroeconomic Performance Assessment:
- GDP growth decomposition: actual vs. potential (output gap); demand
  components (C + I + G + NX); identify whether growth is balanced or
  over-reliant on one driver (e.g., consumption funded by credit)
- Inflation analysis: headline vs. core CPI; decompose into: food,
  energy, services, and goods; inflation expectations anchoring
- Labor market: unemployment rate vs. NAIRU; labor force participation
  trend; real wage growth; productivity growth
- External sector: current account balance (surplus or deficit), its
  sustainability, and financing sources; exchange rate assessment
  (IMF REER and EBA methodology); reserve adequacy

Fiscal Sustainability Assessment:
- Fiscal stance: cyclically adjusted primary balance vs. debt-
  stabilizing primary balance; is fiscal policy procyclical?
- Debt sustainability analysis (DSA): Debt/GDP trajectory under
  base, upside, and stress scenarios; primary balance required for
  debt stabilization; gross financing need (GFN) as % of GDP
- Fiscal risk assessment: contingent liabilities (SOEs, PPPs, sub-
  national governments, state banks); pension system sustainability;
  natural resource revenue dependency

Financial Sector Assessment:
- Banking system health: capital adequacy (Tier 1, NPL ratios),
  profitability (ROA, ROE), and liquidity (LCR, NSFR); systemic banks
  identified; stress test results
- Credit growth: is credit growth outpacing GDP growth? Household
  debt-to-income and corporate debt-to-EBITDA ratios
- Dollarization risk: share of foreign currency loans and deposits;
  currency mismatch in bank balance sheets

Policy Recommendations (IMF Article IV Style):
- Monetary policy recommendation: appropriate policy rate path given
  inflation and growth outlook
- Fiscal policy recommendation: consolidation path; structural
  measures to improve revenue and expenditure quality
- Structural reform priorities: top 3 reforms that would most improve
  medium-term growth potential (business environment, labor market,
  financial sector development)

Format as an IMF Article IV Staff Report Outline with:
  • Macroeconomic Dashboard (Key Indicators vs. Peers)
  • Debt Sustainability Analysis Table
  • Financial Sector Health Scorecard
  • Policy Recommendation Matrix
  • Risk Assessment (External, Domestic, Upside Risks)

My inputs: [COUNTRY NAME | CURRENT GDP AND GROWTH RATE | DEBT/GDP |
            FISCAL DEFICIT | INFLATION RATE | CURRENT ACCOUNT BALANCE |
            CURRENCY REGIME | PRIMARY ECONOMIC CONCERN]

PROMPT 142 — The Moody's Corporate Credit Rating Methodology & Scorecard

Financial Risk Profile (FRP) = issuer rating; explain the scorecard approach for
the specific industry. (2) Business risk factors (typically 50% weight): competitive
position, scale, diversification, operating efficiency, and management quality —
score each sub-factor and determine BRP (Aaa to Ba). (3) Financial risk factors
(typically 50% weight): leverage (Debt/EBITDA), coverage (EBITDA/Interest),
profitability (EBITDA margin), cash flow (FCF/Debt), and liquidity — score each
and determine FRP. (4) Notching adjustments: structural subordination, covenant
protection, collateral, cross-default provisions — up or down from issuer rating.
(5) Liquidity analysis: Moody's liquidity risk assessment (SGL-1 to SGL-4);
key inputs: cash, revolver availability, FCF, near-term maturities, covenant
headroom. (6) Rating sensitivities: what financial metric changes would trigger
a 1-notch upgrade vs. downgrade? (7) Peer comparison: rank the company vs.
3 rated peers on key credit metrics. (8) Rating Committee memo: how to present
the case for a rating action to Moody's Rating Committee.
Format: Credit Rating Report with Scorecard Table, Liquidity Assessment, Peer
Comparison, Sensitivity Analysis, and Rating Committee Memo Outline.
Inputs: [COMPANY NAME | INDUSTRY | LTM REVENUE | LTM EBITDA | TOTAL DEBT |
CASH | CURRENT RATING IF ANY | SPECIFIC RATING QUESTION]

PROMPT 161 — The Renaissance Technologies Statistical Arbitrage Research
Act as Quantitative Researcher at Renaissance Technologies. Design a statistical
arbitrage strategy based on mean-reversion and pairs trading.
Deliver: (1) Statistical arbitrage framework: identify cointegrated pairs using
Engle-Granger or Johansen test; ADF test for stationarity of the spread.
(2) Pairs selection criteria: economic rationale (same industry, same factor exposures),
statistical significance (cointegration p-value < 0.05), and adequate liquidity.
(3) Signal generation: z-score of the spread = (Spread − Mean) / StdDev;
entry at z > 2.0, exit at z = 0, stop-loss at z > 3.5.
(4) Position sizing: dollar-neutral construction (equal $ long and short);
volatility scaling to target constant risk per pair.
(5) Transaction cost modeling: round-trip cost per trade (bid-ask + market impact);
minimum required edge per trade to cover costs.
(6) Portfolio of pairs: correlation between pairs; max pairs in portfolio;
sector concentration limits to prevent factor crowding.
(7) Regime detection: pairs strategies break down in trending markets or when
correlations spike (2008); regime filter using VIX level or rolling correlation.
(8) Backtesting rigor: look-ahead bias prevention; out-of-sample validation;
walk-forward testing across 5 non-overlapping periods.
Format: Stat Arb Strategy Spec with Pairs Screening Methodology, Signal Rules,
Position Sizing, Backtesting Framework, and Regime Filter.
Inputs: [UNIVERSE | HOLDING PERIOD | LEVERAGE | TRANSACTION COST ASSUMPTION |
PAIRS COUNT TARGET | SHARPE TARGET | LOOKBACK FOR COINTEGRATION]

PROMPT 163 — The JPMorgan Credit Derivatives & CDS Strategy

default probability — spread / (1 − Recovery Rate) = annual default probability;
compare CDS-implied rating to agency rating to find mispricing.
(3) CDS index products: CDX IG (125 US IG names), CDX HY, iTraxx Europe, iTraxx
Crossover — index composition, roll schedule, on-the-run vs. off-the-run.
(4) Credit curve trades: CDS curve steepener (short 5yr protection, long 10yr)
when expecting credit improvement; flattener for credit deterioration.
(5) Basis trading: CDS basis = CDS spread − Asset Swap Spread on bond; negative
basis (CDS < bond spread) = buy bond + buy protection; positive basis = sell bond
+ sell protection.
(6) Structured credit derivatives: CDO tranche investing — equity (0-3% attachment),
mezzanine (3-7%), senior (7-15%); correlation sensitivity (equity is long
correlation, senior is short correlation).
(7) CMBX and ABX: CDS on CMBS and ABS pools; the Big Short trade recreated.
(8) Portfolio credit hedging: designing a macro credit hedge using CDX HY for
an IG corporate bond portfolio.
Format: Credit Derivatives Strategy with CDS Mechanics, Single-Name Analysis,

(reverse break fee), and regulatory conditions that would allow walking away.
Format: Target Defense Advisory with Standalone Value Case, White Knight Map,
Poison Pill Analysis, Investor Comm Script, and Negotiation Framework.

PROMPT 213 — The Morgan Stanley Quantitative Research: Factor Zoo Navigation
Act as Head of Quantitative Research at Morgan Stanley. Build a framework
for navigating factor "zoo" — the proliferation of claimed alpha factors.
Deliver: (1) The factor zoo problem: 400+ published factors in academic literature;
most are spurious (p-hacking, data-mining, multiple testing); how to distinguish
genuine factors from noise. (2) Factor validation criteria: (a) Economic intuition —
why should this factor earn a premium? (b) Statistical significance — t > 3.0
after Bonferroni/BHY correction; (c) Out-of-sample evidence (different markets,
different time periods); (d) Low turnover after transaction costs; (e) Capacity.
(3) The 5 robust factors: Market beta, Size (with quality screen), Value (cash flow
based), Momentum (12-1 month), Quality (profitability) — academic consensus.
(4) Factor timing: is factor timing possible? Evidence is weak; factors have
multi-year cycles; overconfident timing destroys returns.
(5) Factor crowding: when factor is too widely known, it underperforms (momentum
crashes); crowding detection methods (factor correlation increase, factor
volatility increase, hedge fund positioning data).
(6) Alternative data factors: satellite imagery, credit card data, NLP — do they
survive transaction cost analysis and institutional capacity constraints?
(7) Factor construction details that matter: value — what metric (P/E vs. P/B vs.
EV/EBITDA vs. FCF yield)? Winsorization level? Universe definition?
Format: Factor Research Framework with Validation Criteria, Robust Factor Summary,
Crowding Detection, Alternative Data Assessment, and Construction Detail Guide.
Inputs: [UNIVERSE | INVESTMENT VEHICLE | CAPACITY CONSTRAINT | FACTOR UNIVERSE |
PRIMARY FACTORS UNDER CONSIDERATION | TRANSACTION COST ASSUMPTION]

PROMPT 233 — The Pimco Mortgage and Securitized Products Strategy

prepayment risk (CPR — Conditional Prepayment Rate); convexity — negative convexity
means duration extends when rates rise, shortens when rates fall (worst of both worlds).
(2) TBA (To-Be-Announced) market: forward delivery mechanism; dollar roll economics
(implied financing rate); specialness and cheapness of TBA vs. specified pools.

(4) CMOs (Collateralized Mortgage Obligations): PAC (Planned Amortization Class) bonds —
stable cashflows within a band; TAC (Targeted Amortization Class); IO/PO strips —
extreme convexity profiles.
(5) Non-agency RMBS: prime jumbo, non-QM (non-Qualified Mortgage), CRT
(Credit Risk Transfer — Fannie/Freddie risk sharing); credit analysis approach
for non-agency (no government guarantee). (6) HELOC and home equity ABS:
2nd lien risk; combined LTV analysis; borrower behavior in HELOCs.
(7) Macro overlay: rate sensitivity of MBS vs. Treasuries; spread widening in
risk-off (MBS are not risk-free despite agency guarantee — convexity selling
pressure); Fed MBS balance sheet runoff impact on spreads.
Format: Mortgage Strategy with Agency MBS Framework, TBA Roll Analysis, Specified
Pool Strategy, Non-Agency DD, and Macro Overlay.

PROMPT 254 — The Hudson Bay Capital Event-Driven Merger Arbitrage
Act as PM at Hudson Bay Capital. Build a systematic event-driven merger
arbitrage strategy.
Deliver: (1) Merger arb mechanics: buy target at current price, short acquirer (for
stock deals); capture spread = deal price − current trading price; annualized return
= spread / days to close × 365. (2) Deal break probability estimation: build a
regression model using historical deal break rates (4-8% for cash deals, 8-15% for
stock deals) with predictors: deal type, size, acquirer financial condition, regulatory
complexity, MAC clause breadth, financing contingency. (3) Break price estimation:
where does target trade if deal breaks? Typically -20 to -35% from deal price;
estimate using: pre-announcement price + strategic value discount + market move.
(4) Risk/reward: spread earned if deal closes (e.g., +4%) vs. loss if deal breaks
(e.g., -25%) × respective probabilities → expected value calculation.
(5) Portfolio construction: 30-50 simultaneous arb positions; sizing inversely
proportional to break probability × break loss; correlation (all arb positions
correlate in a risk-off event when deals cluster to break simultaneously).
(6) Regulatory risk assessment: HSR second request probability; DOJ/FTC challenge
probability based on HHI change and enforcement philosophy; EU Phase II investigation.
(7) Event calendar: FTC/DOJ second request timeline; shareholder vote date; closing
conditions tracking.
Format: Merger Arb Strategy with Break Probability Model, Expected Value Analysis,
Portfolio Construction, Regulatory Risk, and Event Calendar Management.
Inputs: [DEAL NAME | ACQUIRER | TARGET | DEAL PRICE | CURRENT PRICE | SPREAD |
DEAL TYPE (CASH/STOCK) | REGULATORY RISK | EXPECTED CLOSE DATE]

external debt/GDP), (b) Fiscal position (deficit, debt/GDP, revenue base), (c) Growth
prospects, (d) Political/governance risk, (e) Monetary policy credibility.
(3) Spread decomposition: credit spread = expected default loss + risk premium;
compare CDS-implied spread to bond spread (basis); identify mispricing.
(4) Country selection: overweight countries with improving fundamentals (ratings upgrade
candidates); underweight countries with deteriorating dynamics.
(5) Distressed sovereign opportunities: countries in IMF programs or near default;
recovery value analysis; timing entry into distressed credits (pre-IMF program,
post-default, during restructuring).
(6) New issue premium: EM sovereigns typically pay 20-40bps NIC (New Issue Concession);
how to capture NIC through primary market participation.
(7) Risk management: duration vs. benchmark; country concentration limits; liquidity
(bid-ask spreads in illiquid frontier markets); contagion risk.
Format: EM Sovereign Debt Framework with Credit Analysis Template, Spread Decomposition,
Country Selection, Distressed Opportunities, and Risk Management.
Inputs: [BENCHMARK (EMBI GLOBAL/EMBI+) | DURATION TARGET | IG VS. HY SPLIT |
CURRENT COUNTRY OVERWEIGHTS | DISTRESSED EXPOSURE LIMIT | LIQUIDITY REQUIREMENT]

PROMPT 278 — The IMF Monetary Policy Framework Design
Act as Senior IMF Economist advising a central bank on monetary policy
framework design and operational implementation.
Deliver: (1) Monetary policy framework options: (a) Inflation targeting (IT) —
explicit CPI target, instrument independence, transparent communication;
(b) Exchange rate targeting — peg or crawling peg, sacrifices monetary independence;
(c) Money targeting — quantity theory approach, less popular post-1990s;
(d) Average Inflation Targeting (AIT) — Fed's 2020 framework — allows inflation
above target after undershooting.
(2) Inflation target design: point target vs. range; symmetric vs. asymmetric;
headline vs. core vs. supercore; medium-term (2-year) horizon.
(3) Central bank independence: instrument independence (CB sets rates freely) vs.
goal independence (government sets inflation target); legal framework; appointment
process for governor and MPC members; fiscal dominance risk.
(4) Monetary transmission: interest rate channel, exchange rate channel, bank lending
channel, asset price channel — how strong is each channel in this country's context?
(5) Communication framework: forward guidance (calendar-based vs. state-contingent);
inflation report / monetary policy report design; press conference conduct; Fan charts.
(6) Unconventional monetary policy: QE applicability in EM; zero lower bound management;
FX intervention rules (when and how to intervene without compromising IT credibility).
(7) Fiscal-monetary coordination: avoiding fiscal dominance; emergency financing
of government (COVID lessons); fiscal council role.
Format: Monetary Policy Framework Advisory with Framework Options, Target Design,
Independence Governance, Communication Framework, and Unconventional Tools.
Inputs: [COUNTRY | CURRENT FRAMEWORK | INFLATION TARGET | CURRENT INFLATION |
EXCHANGE RATE REGIME | CENTRAL BANK INDEPENDENCE LEVEL | PRIMARY FRAMEWORK CHALLENGE]

plan treatment for each class.

operations during restructuring; roll-up of pre-petition debt; new money terms.
(7) Connected party sales (UK): administrator selling to connected party (management
buyout) requires Phoenix Protocol compliance; independent valuation; marketing evidence.

PROMPT 233 — The Pimco Mortgage and Securitized Products Strategy
Act as PM at PIMCO's Mortgage and Securitized Products team. Build a
comprehensive agency and non-agency mortgage investment framework.
Deliver: (1) Agency MBS: pass-through securities guaranteed by Fannie/Freddie/Ginnie;
prepayment risk (CPR — Conditional Prepayment Rate); convexity — negative convexity
means duration extends when rates rise, shortens when rates fall (worst of both worlds).
(2) TBA (To-Be-Announced) market: forward delivery mechanism; dollar roll economics
(implied financing rate); specialness and cheapness of TBA vs. specified pools.
(3) Specified pool investing: prepayment protection through pool attributes (loan balance,
geography, LTV, credit score); OAS analysis of specified pools vs. TBAs.
(4) CMOs (Collateralized Mortgage Obligations): PAC (Planned Amortization Class) bonds —
stable cashflows within a band; TAC (Targeted Amortization Class); IO/PO strips —
extreme convexity profiles.
(5) Non-agency RMBS: prime jumbo, non-QM (non-Qualified Mortgage), CRT
(Credit Risk Transfer — Fannie/Freddie risk sharing); credit analysis approach
for non-agency (no government guarantee). (6) HELOC and home equity ABS:
2nd lien risk; combined LTV analysis; borrower behavior in HELOCs.
(7) Macro overlay: rate sensitivity of MBS vs. Treasuries; spread widening in
risk-off (MBS are not risk-free despite agency guarantee — convexity selling
pressure); Fed MBS balance sheet runoff impact on spreads.
Format: Mortgage Strategy with Agency MBS Framework, TBA Roll Analysis, Specified
Pool Strategy, Non-Agency DD, and Macro Overlay.
Inputs: [PORTFOLIO TYPE | DURATION TARGET | PREPAYMENT ASSUMPTION | CREDIT QUALITY
CONSTRAINT | AGENCY VS. NON-AGENCY SPLIT | OAS TARGET | LEVERAGE PERMITTED]
