advantages, high ROIC, strong free cash flow generation, and reinvestment opportunities;
avoid commodity businesses, capital-intensive cyclicals, and financial leverage plays.
(2) ROIC analysis: decompose ROIC = NOPAT Margin × Asset Turnover; is ROIC above WACC
and expanding? Why is this company able to earn above-cost returns sustainably?
(3) Competitive advantage assessment: pricing power (has the company raised prices
above inflation?); customer captivity (switching costs quantified); brand value;
scale economics; network effects.
(4) Reinvestment opportunities: what is the reinvestable ROIC? How much of FCF can
be reinvested at above-WACC returns? Companies that can reinvest at high ROIC for
long periods are the most valuable.
(5) Management quality: capital allocation track record (M&A vs. organic investment
vs. buybacks — which has created most value?); insider ownership; long-term incentive
alignment; candid communication.
(6) Valuation: owner earnings yield vs. risk-free rate; intrinsic value as PV of
owner earnings growing at sustainable rate; what growth rate is implied by current price?
(7) Sell discipline: ROIC declining persistently; competitive position eroding;
management quality deteriorating; valuation reaching 3× intrinsic value.
Format: Quality Growth Analysis with ROIC Decomposition, Competitive Advantage
Assessment, Reinvestment Analysis, Management Quality Score, and Intrinsic Value.
Inputs: [COMPANY | SECTOR | LTM ROIC | WACC ESTIMATE | REINVESTMENT RATE |
REVENUE GROWTH | MANAGEMENT TENURE | PRIMARY QUALITY CONCERN]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 247 — The Citigroup M&A Advisory: Hostile Takeover Defense
Act as Senior M&A Advisor at Citi. Advise a Board targeted by an unsolicited
hostile takeover bid.
Deliver: (1) Hostile bid assessment: evaluate the bid's merits (is the price fair?
is the strategic rationale compelling?); determine whether the board's fiduciary duty
requires engagement or allows rejection.
(2) Defense toolkit:
(a) Poison pill (Rights Plan): 20% flip-in trigger; 364-day term; review by ISS
(Glass Lewis typically disfavors pills); Delaware Court validation;
(b) Staggered board: slows control acquisition to 2 years; proxy contest cost.
(c) White knight: approach a preferred acquirer for a competing bid.
(d) Crown jewel defense: agree to divest the most attractive asset to reduce
the hostile bidder's rationale.
(e) Pac-Man defense: counter-bid for the hostile acquirer (rare but legally available).
(3) Shareholder communication: Board letter to shareholders; financial advisor's
opinion on inadequacy of the bid; why the standalone plan is superior.
(4) Proxy contest response: defend director elections against hostile nominees;
identify vulnerable shareholders (index funds are typically vote-by-guidelines);
institutional outreach program.
(5) Legal challenges: Delaware injunction to delay vote; regulatory filing strategy
to slow the process; SEC disclosure challenges if bidder has material non-public info.
(6) Negotiating leverage: use defense mechanisms to create time for a better deal
(higher price, better terms, better strategic partner).
Format: Hostile Defense Advisory with Defense Toolkit Analysis, Shareholder
Communication Plan, Proxy Defense, and Legal Strategy.
Inputs: [TARGET COMPANY | HOSTILE BIDDER | BID PRICE | PREMIUM | CURRENT DEFENSES |
MAJOR SHAREHOLDERS | BOARD COMPOSITION | PRIMARY DEFENSE OBJECTIVE]

PROMPT 249 — The Dimensional Fund Advisors (DFA) Factor Investing Portfolio
Act as Senior Portfolio Manager at Dimensional Fund Advisors. Build a

Deliver: (1) DFA philosophy: implement the insights of Eugene Fama, Kenneth French,
and Merton Miller; evidence-based investing; market efficiency with exploitable
premiums in size, value, and profitability.
(2) Factor premiums: (a) Market (equity risk premium — 5-7% above T-bills historically);
(b) Size (small > large — 2-3% premium historically, diminished recently);
(c) Value (cheap > expensive — B/M, earnings/price — 3-5% premium);
(d) Profitability (high operating profit > low — 3-4% premium);
(e) Investment (conservative investment > aggressive — 2-3% premium).
(3) DFA implementation advantage: patient trading (not chasing factor exposure
at the margin); portfolio reconstitution without rigid rebalancing;
securities lending revenue enhancement.
(4) Portfolio construction: target factor loadings above benchmark; factor tilts
rather than pure factor portfolios; minimize turnover and transaction costs.
(5) Tax management: asset location strategy (factor tilts in tax-deferred accounts);
tax-loss harvesting while maintaining factor exposure.
(6) International factor investing: value and profitability premiums are more
reliably observed internationally; DFA international funds construction.
(7) Performance expectations: factor premium uncertainty (realized premium differs
from expected); multi-decade investment horizon required; the "factor drought" problem.
Format: DFA-Style Portfolio with Factor Premium Evidence, Portfolio Construction,
Implementation Advantages, Tax Strategy, and Performance Expectations.
Inputs: [INVESTOR TYPE | PORTFOLIO SIZE | TAX STATUS | INVESTMENT HORIZON |
FACTOR EMPHASIS (VALUE/SIZE/PROFITABILITY) | INTERNATIONAL WEIGHT | REBALANCING BUDGET]

PROMPT 254 — The Paulson & Co. Event-Driven Merger Arbitrage
Act as Senior PM at Paulson & Co. Build a merger arbitrage investment strategy.
Deliver: (1) Merger arb basics: buy target stock after announcement; earn the "arb
spread" = (Offer Price − Current Price) / Current Price × (annualized based on
time to close); typical annualized returns 8-15%.
(2) Deal probability assessment: determinants of deal completion probability —
strategic rationale, buyer financial capacity, regulatory risk, target board
recommendation, market conditions; assign probability (80%, 90%, 95%).
(3) Risk-reward analysis: Expected Return = P(deal) × spread on completion −
P(deal fails) × downside on failure; downside = target stock returns to pre-announcement
level (typically -20 to -40%); minimum required return given asymmetric risk.
(4) Regulatory risk: antitrust analysis (market share, HHI, DOJ/FTC stance);
CFIUS/NSIA risk for cross-border deals; sector-specific regulatory risk
(banking, telecom, defense — more difficult); EU merger control.
(5) Financing risk: LBO financing commitments; market disruption MAC clause;
financing condition vs. no-financing condition (strongly preferred for arb).
(6) Portfolio construction: position sizing based on deal probability and expected
return; portfolio spread vs. risk; diversification across deal types and sectors;
max single position (typically 5-10% of NAV for high-confidence deals).
(7) Short volatility overlay: merger arb is inherently short volatility; VIX spikes
kill spread compression; how to hedge the vol risk in arb portfolios.
Format: Merger Arb Strategy with Deal Probability Framework, Risk-Reward Analysis,
Regulatory Assessment, Portfolio Construction, and Vol Hedge Design.
Inputs: [DEAL NAME | ACQUIRER | TARGET | DEAL PRICE | ANNOUNCEMENT DATE |
CURRENT SPREAD | REGULATORY CONCERNS | FINANCING STRUCTURE | TIMELINE]

PROMPT 270 — The Citi Structured Equity & Convertible Bonds
Act as Head of Structured Equity at Citi. Design convertible bond and
equity-linked security strategies for issuers and investors.
Deliver: (1) Convertible bond mechanics: bond + embedded call option on issuer stock;
conversion premium (20-35% above current stock price); coupon lower than straight debt
because investor pays for the option.
(2) Issuer rationale: lower cost of capital vs. straight debt; dilution deferred (only
if stock rises); liability management (if stock doesn't rise, it's cheap fixed income).
(3) Investor rationale: asymmetric payoff (participate in equity upside, bond floor
limits downside); delta of embedded option grows as stock rises.
(4) Convertible bond valuation: binomial tree model; OAS of straight bond component;
Black-Scholes for the option component; total value = bond floor + option value.
(5) CB investing strategies: (a) Outright long (balanced risk/return);
(b) Delta-hedged (long vol play — buy CB, short stock); (c) Credit play
(buy distressed CB trading near bond floor, tight spread to recovery value).
(6) Mandatory convertibles (PREPS, DECS): higher coupon, mandatory conversion
at maturity; used by companies to raise equity in disguise.
(7) Market structure: $300B+ US CB market; CB-dedicated funds vs. multi-asset;
liquidity in secondary market (less than straight bonds).
Format: Convertible Bond Strategy with Mechanics, Valuation, Issuer/Investor
Rationale, Investment Strategies, Mandatory Convertibles, and Market Structure.
Inputs: [ROLE (ISSUER/INVESTOR) | COMPANY OR PORTFOLIO | STOCK PRICE | CREDIT RATING |
CONVERSION PREMIUM TARGET | COUPON BUDGET | TENOR | INVESTOR BASE TARGET]

PROMPT 285 — The Allianz Global Investors Convertible Bond Arbitrage
Act as PM at Allianz Global Investors' Convertible Bond team. Build a
convertible bond arbitrage strategy.
Deliver: (1) CB arb mechanics: buy convertible bond (long gamma, long vol) + short
the underlying stock (hedge delta) = delta-neutral position that profits from
realized volatility exceeding implied volatility (gamma scalping).
(2) Theoretical edge: CB implied vol is often lower than listed options implied vol
for the same issuer — buy cheap vol in CB market, sell expensive vol in listed market.
(3) Delta hedging: calculate delta of CB (partial derivative of CB price vs. stock);
short stock at delta to become delta-neutral; re-hedge as stock moves (dynamic delta).
(4) Gamma scalping P&L: each time stock moves significantly, the arb trader buys
low and sells high on the delta hedge — capturing the gamma premium.
(5) Credit risk: the bond component of the CB carries issuer credit risk; credit
hedge via CDS or proxy hedge (short IG credit index if issuer is IG).
(6) Funding and leverage: CB arb funds typically use 2-4× leverage; repo market
for CB funding; short rebate on borrowed stock.
(7) Risk factors: vol compression (implied vol declines, CB cheap on delta basis);
takeover (instant delta=1, eliminates optionality); credit events; short squeeze.
Format: CB Arb Strategy with Delta Hedging Mechanics, Gamma Scalping, Vol Comparison,
Credit Hedge, Funding Structure, and Risk Factors.
Inputs: [PORTFOLIO SIZE | LEVERAGE | VOL TARGET | CREDIT HEDGE APPROACH |
UNIVERSE (US/EU/GLOBAL) | LIQUIDITY CONSTRAINTS | PRIMARY STRATEGY RISK]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

REINSURANCE PROGRAM | CAT EXPOSURE | CAPITAL POSITION | PRIMARY RISK CONCERN]

PROMPT 297 — The Linklaters Private Equity Fund Formation & LP Terms
Act as Partner at Linklaters' Investment Funds group. Design the fund
terms and legal structure for a new PE fund.
Deliver: (1) Fund legal structure: Delaware LP (standard US); Cayman Islands LP
(offshore, tax neutral); Luxembourg SCSp (EU AIFMD compliant); parallel fund
structures for different investor bases.
(2) Key economic terms: management fee (1.5-2% of committed capital during investment period,
reducing to 1-1.25% on invested capital thereafter); carried interest (20%);
hurdle rate (8% preferred return); catch-up (100% GP catch-up to hurdle); waterfall
(European: whole-fund return before carry; American: deal-by-deal carry with clawback).
(3) LP protections: LPAC (Limited Partner Advisory Committee) — consent rights for
conflicts, key man provisions; key man suspension clause (pause investments if key
person departs); no-fault GP removal (75% LP vote); clawback provision.
(4) Investment restrictions: portfolio company size limits; sector and geography limits;
single-investment concentration (max % of fund); leverage limits; co-investment policy.
(5) Subscription mechanics: drawdowns (capital calls); distribution reinvestment period;
commitment period (3-5 years); fund life (10 years + 2 extensions).
(6) Regulatory: AIFMD registration (EU); SEC RIA registration (US); FATCA/CRS for
offshore funds; AML/KYC for LP subscriptions.
(7) Market terms evolution: ESG side letters; most favored nation clauses; fee offsets
from deal fees; subscription credit facilities and LP concerns.
Format: Fund Formation Advisory with Legal Structure, Economic Terms, LP Protections,
Investment Restrictions, Regulatory Compliance, and Market Evolution.
Inputs: [GP NAME | STRATEGY | TARGET SIZE | PRIMARY LP BASE | DOMICILE PREFERENCE |
KEY ECONOMIC TERMS | REGULATORY JURISDICTIONS | PRIMARY LP CONCERN]

PROMPT 298 — The CPPIB Total Portfolio Approach & Risk Factor Framework
Act as Managing Director at Canada Pension Plan Investment Board (CPPIB).
Build a total portfolio approach (TPA) investment framework.
Deliver: (1) CPPIB TPA philosophy: optimize the whole portfolio rather than managing
asset classes in silos; every investment competes for capital against all alternatives
on a risk-adjusted basis; reference portfolio (passive benchmark) as the hurdle.
(2) Risk factor framework: decompose all portfolio positions into underlying risk factors
(equity beta, duration, credit spread, illiquidity premium, inflation, FX);
monitor factor concentration across all assets — private and public.
(3) Absolute vs. relative return focus: TPA focuses on absolute return above the
reference portfolio; active risk budget is the sum of all active positions vs. reference.
(4) Private market integration: PE and infrastructure valued using fair value; how
CPPIB marks-to-market private assets quarterly; private asset factor exposure
mapped to public factor proxies for risk aggregation.
(5) Currency management: CPPIB's global currency hedging program; partial hedge
on non-CAD assets; currency as a separate risk factor allocation.
(6) Sustainable investing: CPPIB's investment-focused approach to ESG
(not exclusions-based); engagement with portfolio companies; climate scenario analysis.
(7) Governance: Total Fund Management (TFM) team; Investment Planning Committee;
Board oversight; performance attribution vs. reference portfolio.
Format: TPA Framework with Risk Factor Model, Private Market Integration, Currency
Strategy, Reference Portfolio Construction, ESG Approach, and Governance.
Inputs: [FUND SIZE | CURRENT ASSET MIX | REFERENCE PORTFOLIO | RISK BUDGET |
PRIVATE MARKET ALLOCATION | CURRENCY POLICY | PRIMARY INVESTMENT OBJECTIVE]

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PROMPT 299 — The Bain Capital Public Equity: Deep Value Stock Analysis
Act as PM at Bain Capital Public Equity. Apply deep value analysis to
identify a materially undervalued public company.
Deliver: (1) Bain's deep value approach: bottom-up, fundamental value investing; buy
businesses trading significantly below intrinsic value with a catalyst for re-rating;
long-term hold (3-7 years); concentrated (15-25 positions).
(2) Sum-of-parts (SOTP) analysis: for conglomerates or complex businesses — value
each business unit separately at appropriate multiple; compare to consolidated
market cap; quantify the conglomerate discount.
(3) Hidden asset analysis: undervalued real estate on balance sheet (below market book
value); IP not reflected in earnings; minority stakes not properly valued by market;
NOL carryforwards with option value.
(4) Normalized earnings power: strip out non-recurring items; adjust for cyclicality
(mid-cycle earnings vs. current-trough earnings); calculate owner earnings.
(5) Catalyst identification: management change; activist involvement; strategic review;
spin-off; asset sale; buyback acceleration; credit rating improvement.
(6) Downside protection: asset coverage (book value, liquidation value); FCF yield;
dividend as a floor; share buybacks providing floor to valuation.
(7) Position sizing: conviction-based sizing; maximum single position 8-10%; how to
build a position without moving the stock (block trading, dark pool).
Format: Deep Value Analysis with SOTP, Hidden Assets, Normalized Earnings, Catalyst
Timeline, Downside Protection, and Position Building Strategy.
Inputs: [COMPANY | TICKER | CURRENT MARKET CAP | BUSINESS SEGMENTS | NORMALIZED
EARNINGS | HIDDEN ASSETS | PRIMARY CATALYST | INTRINSIC VALUE ESTIMATE]

PROMPT 300 — The McKinsey Future of Finance: CFO 2030 Roadmap
Act as Senior Partner at McKinsey. Build a CFO 2030 strategic agenda covering
the transformation of the finance function over the next 5 years.
Deliver: (1) CFO role evolution: from scorekeeper → business partner → value architect;
CFO increasingly owns digital transformation, data strategy, and ESG reporting.
(2) AI and automation roadmap: by 2030, 50-70% of transactional finance tasks automated;
Gen AI for narrative generation, anomaly detection, predictive analytics; identify
finance roles that will be eliminated, transformed, and created.
(3) Real-time finance: from periodic close (monthly/quarterly) to continuous close
and real-time P&L; what technology and process changes enable continuous close.
(4) ESG finance leadership: CFO ownership of ESG reporting (ISSB, CSRD, SEC climate
rules); integrating ESG KPIs into management reporting; sustainability-linked financing.
(5) Finance talent 2030: T-shaped finance professionals (deep technical + broad business);
data scientist integration into FP&A; decline of traditional accountants;
finance as a talent destination for tech-savvy graduates.
(6) Technology stack 2030: cloud ERP (SAP S/4 HANA, Oracle Fusion); AI-native FP&A
(Anaplan, Pigment, Cube); embedded analytics; natural language interfaces for financial data.
(7) Risk and resilience: CFO's expanding risk mandate (cyber, climate, geopolitical,
operational resilience); treasury risk management evolution; real-time risk dashboards.
Format: CFO 2030 Roadmap with Role Evolution, AI Automation Plan, Real-Time Finance,
ESG Leadership, Talent Strategy, Technology Stack, and Risk Framework.
Inputs: [COMPANY REVENUE | INDUSTRY | CURRENT FINANCE MATURITY | CFO TENURE |
DIGITAL INVESTMENT BUDGET | PRIMARY TRANSFORMATION PRIORITY | BOARD EXPECTATIONS]
